Logitech MX Master 4 Special Edition review: Still the productivity mouse to beat, but should you upgrade?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Explore projects, trends and inspiring design work.

Discover design software, tools and creative resources.

Start exploring exclusive deals, expert advice and more.

Packed with digital art tutorials, inspiration and expert advice from ImagineFX.

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A brilliant, comfortable productivity mouse. The haptics and Actions Ring reward creatives who’ll map shortcuts, but Options+ takes work, and for most MX Master 3S owners or non-creatives, it’s a marginal upgrade.

Comfortable, well-judged weight with real desk presence

Special Edition colourway offers a touch of personality

Haptic feedback adds useful tactile confirmation

Actions Ring speeds up app switching and folder access

MagSpeed scrolling is still class-leading

Needs time in Options+ to get the best from it

Marginal upgrade over the MX Master 3S unless you map shortcuts to the Action Ring

Why you can trust Creative Bloq Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Product: Logitech MX Master 4 (Special Edition)

Sensor & Tracking: Darkfield high precision, 200-8000 DPI

Scroll: MagSpeed electromagnetic wheel plus thumb wheel

Connectivity: Bluetooth and Logi Bolt receiver

Software: Logitech Options+ (Windows and macOS)

Best for: creatives, office workers and anyone with long desk sessions

The MX Master 4 Special Edition takes Logitech's excellent black mouse and spruces it up with a new white colourway and red-and-grey accents. Inside, it's the same MX Master 4, and like the 2025 version, it has a tough act to follow. Its predecessor, the MX Master 3S, is already one of the best productivity mice you can buy, so Logitech never intended to reinvent the wheel. Instead, we get more customisation, haptic feedback, and a handful of design tweaks, and in this Special Edition, a more personalised colour choice.

These are all the developments I’d expect from a follow-up to the excellent MX Master 3S, one of the best mice you can buy, but they make this a nicer-looking version of a mouse that was a smart evolution rather than a must-have upgrade, unless, I'd argue, you're a creative; then the Actions Ring becomes a bigger thing than you may expect and is new to MX Master 4, Special Edition or not.

The shape is the same as the MX Master 3S, with its sculpted ergonomic design, deep thumb rest, and plenty of controls to keep your hands off the keyboard. It’s comfortable for long workdays, and the weight is spot on. It has enough heft to feel planted on the desk without feeling clumsy. It’s big, though, and it won’t suit everyone. This is a mouse for your desk, not your laptop bag.

• For more control consider one of the best drawing tablets

The MagSpeed wheel is as good as ever. Flick it, and it free-spins through a long spreadsheet or a 40-page document, then ratchets back to click-by-click scrolling when you need precision. Switching between the two is automatic, which is why it’s so easy to take for granted.

The finish is where things have changed. This is the Special Edition of last year’s MX Master 4 release, so the design changes over the MX Master 3S remain. Logitech has swapped the soft, rubberised coating of older models for something harder, but it’s not slick or glassy. The cover has a nice bit of tooth, and different areas have subtly different textures, so your fingers always know where they are without you looking. It feels different from the old MX Masters. A colleague wasn’t keen on it when they tried [2025’s MX Master 4], but I got used to it quickly, so your mileage may vary, and existing owners may need a few days to adjust.

Those alterations aside, the Special Edition replaces flat blacks with a soft grey-white colour and red accents. The dark grey Actions Ring thumb rest pops in this colourway, and I far prefer it to the faceless black PC-mouse aesthetic of the base model. There’s no price difference between black and Special Edition.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

The thumb rest gets the biggest rethink. It’s usually the most wasted area of a mouse, a bit of plastic to rest your thumb on. Here, it’s the way into the new virtual dial, called the Actions Ring, which makes it feel like part of the design rather than an afterthought.

The haptics are a lovely touch. They kick in when you make a selection and when you open and cycle through the Actions Ring, so I can keep my eyes on the screen, click and scroll, and still feel a little resistance and feedback. This also helps with gesture control, too, if you go as far as needing that kind of input. It’s subtle, but it makes the mouse feel more connected to what you’re doing.

As you may have guessed, the Actions Ring is the real star of the new features. It’s an on-screen dial that expands what the mouse can do, making it easy to open folders or switch between apps without dropping into the Windows menus. You can tailor the ring to the job, from something as basic as Google to Photoshop or DaVinci Resolve. Support isn’t universal, though. The best digital art and design apps like Krita, Affinity Photo and CorelDRAW are covered, but smaller apps like Escape Motions Rebelle aren’t a default option, so you’ll need a workaround: assign function keys in Options+, then match them to the app’s own shortcuts for undo, clear layer or brush toggles. It works, but you need to know your keys or set them up ahead of time.

This also means getting to grips with Options+ and its app features, which is a lot of learning for a mouse. I’d like it to work more without that software running in the background. But if you persevere, it adds real depth and makes the mouse faster to use and more flexible. Once it’s set up, you’ll love it. You can tweak everything from haptic strength to scroll speed, and I’d probably be moaning if I couldn’t fiddle.

The haptics, gestures, and Actions Ring are a step beyond the MX Master 3S, and creatives who map shortcuts to the ring will get the most from this mouse.

So, should you buy the MX Master 4 Special Edition? If you’re coming from a basic mouse, absolutely. The ergonomics, scrolling and customisation make it a brilliant tool for anyone who works at a computer all day, whether in creative apps or simply navigating menus and Google.

If you already own an MX Master 3S, it comes down to how you work. The haptics and Actions Ring are genuinely useful and, for creative apps, a new, easier way to work, so it’s worth it if you’ll map shortcuts to the ring or, like me, hate sorting through Windows menus and would rather one-click into the regular apps and files. For everyone else, they’re not enough to make a perfectly good mouse obsolete.

Your AI agent is only as good as your endpoint data

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

PARTNER CONTENT: Real-time endpoint data keeps AI-driven IT operations accurate, fast and under control

Every so often a flaw comes along that shows how fast the ground can shift under IT and security teams. PHP2Shell, a pair of critical flaws in PHP, was one of them. Details leaked before the planned disclosure, frontier AI helped researchers find the flaws, and PHP shipped fixes the next day. For many teams, though, the patch was the easy part. So what does it take to respond at that speed, and where should teams begin?

Picture a loop. Software finds where a risk lives and ranks what matters most. It proposes a fix, then, inside limits people define, carries it out and checks that it worked. Routine, low-risk actions can run on their own. Higher-impact ones wait for a person to approve. A few are never handed over at all. Each step leaves a record that someone can review and reverse.

Three problems keep coming up. The first is visibility. PHP2Shell reportedly sat undetected for more than a decade. For these two flaws, the fix was a version upgrade. The hard part was finding every copy of PHP, including copies bundled inside other software or extracted by hand onto Windows machines that never registered as installed applications. Then teams had to work out which copies the internet could reach and check for signs of earlier attack. An inventory built from a periodic snapshot can't answer those questions on the day they matter.

The second is tool-hopping. Answering "where does this run, and can the internet reach it?" often means pivoting across an asset inventory, a vulnerability scanner and an external attack surface tool, each with its own view. Every hop adds delay. Every console is another chance to disagree with the last.

The third is pace. Several security firms reported exploitation attempts before PHP released its fixes, and Cloudflare reported early attacks hours ahead of the patch. Frontier AI models helped find PHP2Shell, and in April Anthropic said its Claude Mythos Preview model had found thousands of previously unknown flaws across every major operating system and browser. With models like these in the picture, the old rhythm of disclose, patch and verify assumes a gap that keeps shrinking.

Put an AI agent on top of stale, fragmented data and these problems don't go away. They scale.

Endpoints are where work happens and where many attacks end up, so they are the ground truth for any decision about the estate. Four qualities make that data a sound foundation for autonomous action:

Accuracy: Live data from the endpoints themselves, not a snapshot that was true last week.

Speed: A short path from question to action, with no hopping between tools to reconcile answers.

Control: Approvals and an audit trail, plus the ability to roll back, so any automated action can be reviewed and reversed.

Shared truth: IT operations, security and the system of record working from one view, rather than arguing over whose data is right.

Matt Psencik, a threat research engineer at Tanium, put the visibility problem bluntly when PHP2Shell broke: "The installations that worry me most are the ones a security team doesn't know it owns."

AI sits on both sides of this. It helped researchers find the PHP2Shell flaws, and attackers can use it to build exploits too. For defenders, AI works best as the operator and the interface: it takes a plain-language question, works out what to ask of the data, and proposes or carries out the next step. But it is only as good as the data it is grounded in. A fluent answer built on outdated information is still wrong, just more confidently so. Keeping people in the loop where stakes are high is what makes automation safe to expand.

Teams don't need to hand over the keys all at once. Start here:

Find every copy: Don't stop at the installed-applications list. PHP2Shell showed that software bundled inside other products can slip past it. Compare your CMDB against what is really on your network and record exact versions.

Draw the lines: Rank systems by what the internet can reach, then decide which response actions run automatically, which need approval and which are never delegated. Write it down before an agent needs it.

Pilot one low-risk workflow: Pick something contained, build approval into it, and read the audit trail before widening the scope.

Teams can also shorten the pilot with a partner. World Wide Technology (WWT), a Tanium partner, works with many large organizations on technology rollouts and hosts a Tanium lab in its Advanced Technology Center, where customers can test an approach hands-on before moving it into production.

That second problem, the hopping, is the one Tanium Atlas was built to close. Instead of moving between consoles, an operator asks a plain-language question, gets an answer from live endpoint data, and acts on it inside limits the team sets, with every action recorded. Want to see how it works in practice? Click here and set up time to see Tanium in action.

SPONSORED POST: Knowing where your data lives is only part of the challenge. Who controls it, who can access it and how quickly you can recover when things go wrong matter just as much.

The Sky Today on Thursday, November 12: An open cluster in Perseus

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

The constellation Perseus is famous for its bright Double Cluster, but these two open clusters aren’t the only groups of young stars in the Hero worth viewing. Today, let’s shift our gaze to the western portion of this constellation, near its border with Andromeda, to check out M34, a mid-5th-magnitude open cluster containing about 100 stars.

Sitting 5.2° west-northwest of 2nd-magnitude Algol (Beta [β] Persei), M34 spans about half a degree, roughly the size of the Full Moon. In physical space, astronomers believe the group spans about 14 light-years and sits 1,400 light-years away. It’s best seen under lower magnifications, such as in binoculars or small amateur scopes (4 inches or so). You’ll spot several dozen stars brighter than 12th magnitude, with its brightest suns spread throughout the cluster.

Sunrise: 6:42 A.M.Sunset: 4:46 P.M.Moonrise: 10:08 A.M.Moonset: 6:57 P.M.Moon Phase: Waxing crescent (14%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

InfoQ Homepage News LinkedIn Replaces Argo CD with a Custom GitOps Engine for Kubernetes Deployments

Nov 12, 2026 4 min read

In a recent post, LinkedIn revealed it has replaced Argo CD with its own GitOps engine. This new system deploys applications across over 250 Kubernetes clusters. It supports more than 4,000 services and manages over 100,000 deployments each week. According to a post, deployment infrastructure failures fell from 925 to 63 despite a 3.7x increase in deployment volume. The median time to detect and conclude a failed deployment dropped from 16.1 to 2.45 minutes.

LinkedIn started with one Argo CD instance per cluster, a topology it chose because Argo CD already managed its cluster add-ons that way. Clusters run up to 5,000 nodes each. A central deployment orchestrator spawned an ephemeral pod per deployment, with Argo CD hidden behind it. The team lists seven problems that emerged at scale. Argo CD doesn't have an observedGeneration, so you can't link sync and health status to a specific deployment. LinkedIn created a Lua health check that uses a tracking annotation to address this. A Synced and Healthy application could also resolve to an unintended revision when stale repo-server cache entries were used.

Two of the issues were architectural. Argo CD maintains one watch per group-version-resource across Applications. LinkedIn’s clusters have low resource-type diversity. This means etcd churn from one app can slow syncs for all apps. In one test, sync took under 3 minutes on a cluster with 60,000 pods and over 10 minutes with more than 80,000. The second was server-side-apply PATCH. Kubernetes truncates managedFields once an object has had more than 10 distinct field managers, and the API server then forgets who owns a field. In LinkedIn's AI workloads, which run multiple containers per Pod, a container removed from a manifest could therefore survive on the live Pod. The team had pruning disabled because destructive operations in production require approval. The remaining problems were CRD schema changes that required controller restarts across the fleet. Some OutOfSync states did not reflect real drift. Error messages were buried in boilerplate.

LinkedIn evaluated Flux but found it shared the same controller paradigm, so it built the engine as a library. Each deployment runs in its pod, with no shared controller state. Updates use PUT for full replacements, not PATCH. They retry on resourceVersion conflicts with exponential backoff. A 3-way diff across the live object, the previously deployed manifest, and the newly rendered manifest detects drift, and genuine drift fails the deployment. The same pod then watches the object's status until it converges or times out, and the API server unwraps errors for the user. Objects missing from the rendered manifest become deletion candidates, subject to delete protection, and convergence logic for LinkedIn's CRDs is built in.

The comparison uses the same calendar month a year apart. The migration ran from July 2025 to April 2026, which makes May 2026 the first clean post-migration month. The infra failure count covers only failures caused by the deployment system and excludes drift errors, since the two systems define drift differently. LinkedIn states that the improvement isn't solely due to the new engine. The orchestrator's error classification and reliability also got better during this time. The median duration for successful deployments is similar for both systems. However, the mean and tail have improved. The team attributes this to a namespace-scoped informer cache.

The engine is internal, and no open-source release is mentioned. Argo CD remains in use at LinkedIn for cluster add-ons and system services. The team had also found that maintaining 200+ Argo CD instances made each upgrade a multi-week project.

Teams running Argo CD at scale can check two things without a rewrite. Count the field managers on long-lived objects, since managedFields truncation can leave orphaned containers that produce no error. Then check whether sync latency tracks total pod count on shared clusters, which points to watch contention. LinkedIn concludes that the build-or-extend decision relies on the operational model. They say that per-deployment isolation, not controller tuning, allows the system to scale with compute.

LinkedIn is not the only large operator to build deployment logic around Kubernetes rather than adopt a general-purpose GitOps controller. Uber’s Up platform assigns each deployment to a dedicated controller. This is the Uber Deployment Controller. It translates the intent into Kubernetes primitives and sends status updates. This helps the workflow move forward. Uber keeps a long-running controller, while LinkedIn runs one ephemeral pod per deployment with no shared controller state.

Airbnb went further back and built its own tooling years earlier. In a talk at QCon London 2019 on InfoQ, infrastructure engineer Melanie Cebula introduced kube-gen. This internal framework creates Kubernetes configuration from one versioned project file. It also includes a deploy tool that applies or replaces objects and restarts pods, so changes take effect right away. Airbnb's deploy tool also returns an atomic success or failure, and a container that restarts for 30 seconds at the start of a rollout counts as a failed deploy. Airbnb was then at roughly 125,000 production deploys a year.

Palantir Grew Revenue 96%. The Stock Fell 5%. Here's What I'm Doing.

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Palantir (PLTR +2.12%) reported very strong earnings once again on Nov. 2. The highlight of the release was its accelerating 96% revenue growth. However, the market didn't love the results, and the stock sold off more than 10% in the days following earnings.

The stock has rebounded some, but recently was still down 5% from its price before earnings were released. Overall, Palantir is down around 20% from its all-time high, so some investors may be viewing this as a buying opportunity.

But what am I doing? I'm still staying patient. Palantir is a great business that's growing remarkably fast, but there's one huge holdup that keeps me from purchasing shares, and it could be some time before I decide to open a position.

At face value, Palantir's AI analytics business is doing fantastic. Its revenue is soaring, and it has 49% profit margins. The business is strong, and so is its outlook, so what's not to love about this stock? It all comes down to the price tag.

The best companies bought at the wrong price can still be bad investments, and that's exactly how I view Palantir's stock. Palantir trades at about 100 times this year's earnings and 67 times next year's earnings.

For reference, I can buy other AI stocks, like Nvidia or Alphabet, which trade at 11 and 19 times next year's earnings, respectively. These two are stalwarts in the industry, yet trade at huge discounts. Palantir's growth will be dedicated to reducing its valuation over the next few years, while Nvidia and Alphabet are cheap enough that every bit of growth they deliver should result in a corresponding rise in their stock.

That makes the long-term prospects for these two (and countless other AI investments) far stronger. As a result, I'm OK passing on Palantir. There are many great AI investments, and you don't have to invest in them all. The day of reckoning for Palantir's stock valuation could be coming soon, as it has maintained its high valuation even while many other AI stocks have seen their valuation premiums erased. Time will tell, but right now it seems like a precarious time to be a Palantir investor, with so many other great deals available in AI stocks.

I'd consider buying Palantir stock at around 30 to 40 times forward earnings, but we're a huge sell-off away from seeing those levels.

GITEX GLOBAL 2026 moves to Expo City Dubai with new dates and a summit

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Huawei, Microsoft, AWS and AMD headline this year’s line-up, with robotics, cybersecurity and data centres getting their own showcases. Joby Aviation, Workday and Nexthink make their debut.

GITEX GLOBAL 2026 returns to Dubai this December with new dates, a new venue and a new format. A one-day summit opens the event on 7 December at Dubai World Trade Centre. A four-day expo follows from 8 to 11 December at Dubai Exhibition Centre in Expo City Dubai.

The organisers have named Huawei, Microsoft, Snowflake, Tencent Cloud, Alibaba Cloud, HCL Software, Telnyx, Intel, AWS and AMD among the companies headlining this year. First-time exhibitors include Joby Aviation, Workday and Nexthink.

The GITEX Summit is a full-day meeting of ministers, policymakers, tech leaders, investors and founders, according to the organisers. It runs from 9:30am to 5pm in Sheikh Maktoum Hall at Dubai World Trade Centre.

The programme is built around three themes: the compute economy, the new map of capital and future frontiers.

Speakers announced so far include Peng Xiao, CEO of G42, Emaar founder Mohamed Alabbar and DigitalBridge CEO Marc Ganzi. New York University emeritus professor Gary Marcus, Sensofusion’s Mikko Hyppönen and ControlAI’s Connor Leahy are also on the list, alongside deputy ministers from Poland and Azerbaijan and the ITU’s deputy secretary general.

The expo moves to Dubai Exhibition Centre and runs from 10am to 5pm each day. The organisers expect more than 6,000 exhibitors from more than 80 countries. They bill it as the world’s largest tech and AI show. They have framed this edition under the theme “The Global Reset 2027”. Expand North Star and LIVX by GITEX run alongside it at the same venue.

Huawei, a Platinum Sponsor, arrives after unveiling a next-generation AI computing architecture designed to connect up to one million AI processors. TNW covered its SuperPoD plans at Huawei Connect in September.

Microsoft, a Gold Sponsor, is accelerating its $15.2 billion investment in the UAE. That includes 200MW of new AI computing capacity with G42, due online by the end of 2026. G42’s ties to the US have shaped its chip access this year.

AWS will bring Amazon Bedrock, which the organisers say supports generative AI applications at more than 100,000 organisations. AMD will show its Instinct accelerators and EPYC processors for hyperscale data centres.

Joby Aviation makes its GITEX GLOBAL debut as Dubai prepares for its first commercial electric air taxi service. Nexthink, a $3 billion unicorn, is also a first-timer; its digital employee experience technology now reaches more than 20 million employees.

Workday, whose HR and finance platform is used by more than 65% of Fortune 500 companies, debuts too. So does Riello UPS, a European maker of uninterruptible power systems that scale up to 1,600 kVA.

Snowflake has a $6 billion strategic collaboration with AWS. Lenovo is also on the list, arriving after announcing an AI Cloud Gigafactory built on Nvidia’s Vera Rubin architecture.

On stage, Dr Tony Han, founder and CEO of WeRide, joins the speaker line-up. WeRide launched Dubai’s first fully driverless, fare-charging robotaxi service with Uber, the same pairing that took its robotaxis to Madrid. It plans to deploy 1,200 robotaxis across the Gulf by 2027. Patrick Johansson, Ericsson’s head of Europe, Middle East and Africa, is speaking too.

GITEX WORLD RobotiX is the event’s showcase for physical AI: machines that sense their surroundings and act in the real world. It brings together live robotics demos, product launches, buyer meetings, pilot project talks and investor conversations. Its focus extends to vision, simulation, fleet orchestration, cybersecurity and connectivity.

Cyber Valley gathers security companies including Cloudflare, Fortinet, Netskope, Palo Alto Networks, Proofpoint, Trend AI, Veeam and Zscaler. Talks will cover agentic AI threats, machine-speed defence, sovereign security and protecting critical infrastructure. The Cyberbase Village adds live hacking challenges and hands-on workshops.

The data centre programme follows what the organisers call a race worth nearly $3 trillion in investment. Close to 100 GW of new capacity is expected worldwide by 2030. Abu Dhabi’s planned 5 GW UAE-US AI Campus is part of that build-out, and Gulf investment money is already moving into the sector. Sessions will cover gigawatt-scale projects, high-performance compute, energy resilience, advanced cooling and sovereign infrastructure.

Registration for GITEX GLOBAL 2026 is open on the GITEX website. The summit takes place on 7 December at Dubai World Trade Centre, and the expo runs from 8 to 11 December at Dubai Exhibition Centre, Expo City Dubai.

A visitor pass covers the expo, Expand North Star, LIVX and the free conferences, but not the summit. Summit access comes with the GITEX Summit, Premium and All Access passes. Online prices for the visitor pass apply until 7 December.

Lazio, derby da record: incasso stimato vicino ai 4 milioni

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

La previsione supera i tre milioni incassati contro il Bayern nel 2024; gli abbonati sono 4.200.

La Lazio potrebbe incassare circa 4 milioni di euro dalla vendita dei biglietti per il derby con la Roma di domenica 13 dicembre, in programma alle 12.30. Secondo quanto riportato da Il Messaggero, La stima, riportata da Il Messaggero, collocherebbe la partita al primo posto nella storia degli incassi al botteghino del club. La cifra potrebbe essere anche leggermente superiore.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

La previsione è legata alla presenza di appena 4.200 abbonati: per questa gara, gran parte delle presenze sugli spalti si tradurrebbe quindi in biglietti acquistati. La società, da anni, non diffonde i dati relativi agli incassi al botteghino.

Il confronto più recente è con il derby del 21 settembre 2025, quando la Lazio incassò 2,2 milioni di euro. In quell’occasione, però, gli abbonati erano quasi trentamila. Per la prossima sfida con la Roma, la stima si avvicina invece ai quattro milioni.

Un incasso di quell’entità supererebbe nettamente i 3 milioni di euro registrati il 14 febbraio 2024, nella gara di andata degli ottavi di Champions League contro il Bayern. Quella sera la Lazio allenata da Sarri vinse 1-0.

La partita con il Bayern aveva già prodotto un incasso superiore di quasi un milione rispetto al debutto nella stessa competizione contro l’Atletico Madrid, il 19 settembre 2023. La sfida terminò in pareggio grazie al gol del portiere Provedel.

Should You Buy Anthropic IPO Stock? Here’s What History Says

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic may begin trading as early as next week.

Investors have been sharply focused on initial public offerings this year, and the next such operation may be launched by AI lab Anthropic. Why are investors so interested in IPOs right now? They've offered the opportunity to get in on some of the most exciting companies in the high-growth artificial intelligence (AI) space. AI is booming now, and the market is expected to reach into the trillions by the end of the decade.

Cerebras Systems (CBRS +7.92%) launched its offering in May, the biggest operation in 2026 up to that moment. Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -2.33%) a month later completed the largest IPO ever, raising more than $85 billion and entering the market with a value surpassing $2 trillion.

Now, Anthropic aims to price on Nov. 18 and begin trading on Nov. 19, CNBC reported, citing people familiar with the situation. Should you participate in the Anthropic IPO? Let's take a look at what history says.

Before we look at historical trends, though, we'll first take a quick look at the Anthropic story so far and what we know about the upcoming operation. Anthropic develops the Claude family of models, and its founders, led by chief Dario Amodei, originally worked at rival AI lab OpenAI.

The company has seen tremendous success in terms of demand and revenue, going from zero in sales three years ago to more than $17 billion in the third quarter of this year. And in the first nine months of the year, revenue soared 1,200% year over year to $33.2 billion. Enterprise adoption of Claude has been tremendous, with 11,900 customers accounting for $100,000 or more in revenue on a trailing 12-month basis. These customers use Claude for a variety of projects, from the simple to the complex, and Claude products may be applied to industry-specific tasks such as robotics or drug discovery.

This is positive, but Anthropic also involves some risk. It's not yet profitable, with a net loss of $104 billion in the first nine months of this year, and the AI lab pointed out in about 90 pages of its prospectus many potential problems that could arise. These include regulatory and litigation risk, and the idea that as AI models become more advanced, the possibility of doing harm grows too.

Anthropic this week said that it would offer 825 million shares at an IPO price between $70 and $78, according to a filing. People familiar with the situation told CNBC that Anthropic aims to allocate 20% of its IPO shares to retail investors -- as SpaceX did during its IPO.

This sets Anthropic and SpaceX apart from traditional IPOs, in which institutional investors generally have access to most of the shares, and retail investors get in on 5% to 10%. At the highest pricing, Anthropic could raise more than $64 billion, and the company may enter the market with a trillion-dollar valuation.

Should you participate in this IPO? Let's take a look back in time to see whether IPOs have delivered big returns in their early days of trading.

A look at the 10 biggest IPOs from 1999 through 2023, including companies such as Rivian and Coupang, shows that eight out of 10 fell in their first three months of trading. And this year's major IPOs followed that trend, with Cerebras declining 29% and SpaceX slipping 6% in their first three months on the market.

So, history shows us that buying a stock during an IPO isn't absolutely necessary to get in on a potentially big growth story. In fact, in many cases, investors would have been better off buying shares later on the dip. This means that, if you're on the fence regarding whether you should buy Anthropic right out of the gate, you don't have to rush to make a decision. If history is right, other opportunities to buy this AI growth player are likely to arise in the months and years ahead.

Advent Calendars for Gardeners

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

The daily treat dedicated gardeners deserve! From charming botanical illustrations to a block-built flower bouquet, seeds to sow in spring or a barbecue grill set, a garden-themed advent calendar will fill your Christmas countdown full of plant-based joy.

Is the Crypto Market Walking Into a Bull Trap? Bitcoin ETF Records Five Straight Days of Outflow

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Bitcoin’s latest recovery attempt is running into a serious problem: institutional investors are pulling money out of the market. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETF recorded five consecutive days of net outflows, shedding $1.44 billion between November 5 and November 11.

Bitcoin is now trading near $73,500, struggling to reclaim the $75,000 level. Bitcoin ETF outflow streak raises a bigger question: Is the crypto market walking into another bull trap?

The timing is particularly concerning. Bitcoin briefly reclaimed $80,000 on November 4 before reversing sharply the following day. Crypto has since struggled to regain momentum, leaving us questioning buyers’ decisions and whether we are stepping in too early.

The latest Bitcoin ETF withdrawals included $430.2 million on November 5, followed by another $411.9 million on November 6. Outflows continued on November 9, 10, and 11.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) accounted for most of the selling pressure. The fund alone recorded $269.7 million in net outflows yesterday.

The latest Bitcoin ETF outflow streak shows that institutional demand is struggling to support the market’s recovery. Five consecutive sessions of withdrawals have removed $1.44 billion from U.S. spot Bitcoin funds. BlackRock’s IBIT accounted for three-quarters of that amount.

However, institutional demand has not disappeared entirely. Morgan Stanley’s Bitcoin ETF attracted $9 million yesterday, offering a small counterpoint to the ETF-wide selling.

Glassnode’s latest Week 46 report adds another warning. Bitcoin has fallen below the short-term holder cost basis of $75,600. This leaves recent buyers sitting on unrealized losses.

The research firm also points to shrinking stablecoin supply and continued ETF selling. Both developments display that fresh capital is struggling to enter the market.

This creates an uncomfortable setup for anyone buying the latest dip. A short-lived recovery could attract retail investors before another wave of selling pushes prices lower.

Bitcoin’s derivatives market offers another piece of the puzzle. BTC futures open interest remains above $50 billion, while funding rates remain positive. This number somehow paints a clear picture: leveraged traders are still paying to maintain bullish positions.

However, leverage has already started coming out of the market. Open interest has declined, and much of the long-side leverage near current prices has been flushed.

That creates two possible outcomes. If spot demand returns, reduced leverage could help Bitcoin recover without triggering another major liquidation cascade. Alternatively, another failed breakout could leave remaining leveraged buyers exposed to further downside.

For now, $75,600 is the important recovery threshold. Reclaiming that level would put Bitcoin back above the short-term holder cost basis and improve market sentiment.

A grinding climb toward $80,000 would offer stronger evidence that buyers are regaining control, but losing the $72,000 area could expose BTC to another test of $70,000.

The bull trap argument also has a counterpoint. There is accumulation among two large wallet cohorts, while selling momentum has started weakening. This shows that some investors are already positioning for a recovery.

The market does not need another dramatic crash to trap investors. Sometimes, all it takes is a convincing bounce that runs out of buyers.

Former Rangers manager quits role after mirroring disastrous Sunderland record in 2958 day low

Former Rangers head coach Michael Beale has quit as manager of Belgian Pro League side Zulte Waregem just weeks after offering fans a refund following a heavy away defeat.

According to reports, Beale has resigned after just 177 days at the Elindus Arena, after the 4-1 defeat to Sint-Truidense saw them fall to their sixth consecutive defeat, matching their worst run of results since October 7, 2018. The ex-Rangers man leaves them just four points clear of the relegation zone.

His first managerial role since being sacked by Sunderland almost three years ago, Beale is understood to have fallen on his sword due to a breakdown in his relationship with senior figures over the club’s direction and strategy. The 46-year-old’s time started brightly as they went unbeaten in their opening five games. However, their form quickly unravelled, and they have been without a win since September 6.

"I will sit down with the board during the international break and we will put our heads together,” said Beale at the weekend. "Will I still be here when we return for our next match? A lot depends on how the discussions go. Last summer, we made a decision to concentrate on young players, but we have to accept that this group now needs help. We can't deny we are in trouble."

While the club are yet to officially confirm Beale’s departure, numerous reports say he has informed the club of his decision to depart. Bizarrely, his poor run of results means that he leaves his role with an identical record to his ill-fated spell at the Stadium of Light, where he also recorded four wins, two draws, and six losses, resulting in a 33.3% win rate after 12 games at both clubs.

Appointed by Zulte Waregem on May 19, he offered to refund fans’ ticket and travel costs following a heavy 3-0 defeat to La Louviere last month, approaching supporters at the final whistle to inform them of the reimbursement in person.

The ex-Ibrox boss first linked up with Rangers as part of the coaching staff during Steven Gerrard’s three-and-a-half-year reign in charge of the club, working as Rangers’ first-team coach as they went unbeaten to win the Scottish Premiership title in the 2020/21 season. He joined the former Liverpool icon in departing the club shortly afterwards, with the pair moving to Aston Villa in November 2021.

After leaving the Midlands upon Gerrard’s sacking shortly afterwards, Beale moved into his first managerial role with EFL Championship side Queens Park Rangers the following summer, but was poached by Rangers as a replacement for Giovanni van Bronckhorst just five months into his spell at Loftus Road, and was named the Ibrox club’s 18th permanent manager in their history in November 2022.

He was unable to replicate his previous success at Rangers though, and struggled in the main role at Ibrox. A mixed start to his tenure, saw him win record eight wins, two draws and four defeats. A 3-1 home defeat to Aberdeen sealed his fate though, with the club opting to bring an end to his time in the dugout after a third defeat in seven games. Appointed as manager of Sunderland 15 months later, his time on Wearside was a disaster, and the club parted ways with him after just nine weeks in the role, where he was succeeded by Regis Le Bris.

New warning sent to Fire Stick users as police 'trawl through bank accounts' to contact illegal EFL streamers

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Customers of jailbroken Amazon Fire TV Stick devices have received a huge new warning over such practices as authorities continue to push forward in their bid to tackle the issue, with the police now taking fresh action.

It's well documented, of course, that many people up and down the country purchase illegal Fire Stick devices in order to avoid forking out for controversially-priced subscription packages from the likes of Sky Sports and TNT Sports.

A central role of this illegal market is the ability to stream live sporting events through the chipped software, which often works out significantly cheaper than a £30 monthly Sky Sports subscription, for example.

Indeed, a fair share of 'dodgy' Fire Stick customers will just happen to be EFL supporters, too, as the devices often enable users to bypass the 3pm broadcasting blackout on Saturday afternoons - making it much easier to watch their team in action.

Many are far more content with paying between £30 and £100 for an illegal device as opposed to a legal subscription package, much to the ire of the authorities.

Ultimately, though, it's against the law, and recent years have seen big-hitting broadcasters and the authorities alike join forces in an effort to prevent the ever-growing popularity of jailbroken Fire Stick devices.

Those found guilty of infiltrating the devices with illegal software for distribution and monetary gain purposes run the risk of landing behind bars, but it's not only the sellers that can find themselves on the wrong side of the law and a new warning has now been sent to anybody considering buying a pirated Fire Stick.

The police have attempted various measures in their ongoing battle to crack down on illegal broadcasting distribution and consumption over the last couple of years, but they're now changing their strategy in a development that shows just how serious the issue really is.

Ex-detective Ray Wilding recently spoke on the BBC's Morning Live show to reveal a fresh strategy from the authorities which will increase their chances of taking action against users as well as distributors.

Wilding disclosed that police have now decided to begin tracing the details found in the bank accounts of sellers, which will show exactly who has been purchasing 'dodgy' Fire Stick devices.

He explained: "They’re going for the distributors. They’re going for the sellers and they are having a lot of success.

"But actually, once they’ve got the seller, they’re going through their accounts. They’re seeing who has been paying the money for these illegal things, and if they’ve been paying them for these sticks then people should possibly expect a conversation or two if the police want to know why they’ve been paying them money for this.

"It’s a crime. It’s not allowed. Owning the stick itself is not a crime, but using it for this purpose is.

"They (subscription service providers) are losing money to this, so they have been reaching out and are taking action against those people using them illegally as well."

Much of the authorities' efforts have revolved around taking root of the problem and arresting individuals who are hijacking the devices with illegal software to distribute to the public, which has resulted in jail sentences.

Now, however, they're becoming increasingly interested in those keeping the illegal industry afloat by knowingly purchasing such devices, and simply buying a 'dodgy' Fire Stick can still land you in serious trouble.

Wilding himself has warned of this by adding: "I wouldn’t say it’s something you should do.

"It is actually a crime and it can have a nasty fine, a huge fine and also a custodial sentence for users and sellers of these things."

Football League World assesses and ranks the FIFTEEN best away fanbases in the EFL right now

It has been widely reported that penalties such as a hefty £50,000 fine can be imposed upon anybody found guilty of supplying or simply watching illegally-streamed software, and this new form of action will make it much easier to track down users.

The most significant legal action will always, quite naturally, be brought against the individuals chipping and distributing 'dodgy' Fire Sticks in the first place, but it's clear that the police are more intent than ever to clamp down on users and this is simply the latest effort to be made.

That's because, earlier this year, the Federation Against Copyright Theft (FACT) announced that contact had been made with more than a thousand individuals across the UK warning them to 'immediately cease using illegal TV streaming services' in order to avoid the possibility of prosecution under Section 11 of the Fraud Act 2006.

FACT's campaign emphasised that users are not anonymous, with chairman Kieron Sharp having previously warned individuals of "potential criminal liability" and "exposure to scams, viruses and harmful content."

Arrow keys or WASD to move. P to pause, M to mute.

Meanwhile, a court order back in March saw Revolut provide the names and addresses of more than 300 'dodgy' Fire Stick users to Sky, who have become involved in the investigations alongside the authorities in a joint effort to bring sellers and consumers alike to justice.

Hyperliquid (HYPE) is gaining attention as a decentralized trading platform focused on perpetual futures. The protocol operates without traditional onboarding barriers and offers access to assets such as BTC, ETH, SOL, AVAX, and SUI without requiring ownership of the underlying tokens.

Its infrastructure includes the HyperBFT consensus mechanism, designed to support high-speed transactions. As platform activity grows, market participants are assessing the HYPE Price outlook for 2026 and beyond.

The daily price chart shows Hyperliquid in a strong, sustained uptrend since 2025, largely driven by exchange utilities and frequent ecosystem updates. These developments continue to fuel long-term demand, encouraging institutional investors and whales to steadily accumulate HYPE.

Looking at price history, HYPE traded near a low of $9.00–$10.00 in April 2025 before surging to a local peak of $59.00 in September 2025. An aggressive correction followed, pulling the price down to a low of $21.00 in January 2026. After establishing a bottom at that level, HYPE rallied to break its previous ceiling and hit an all-time high of $76.00 in June 2026.

That record was subsequently broken as buying momentum extended through July and August, pushing the token to a new all-time high of $86.00. In early September 2026, HYPE briefly reached a record peak of $89.35, but by mid-September, it took a healthy pull back as it retested support at $76.00. But, after mid-September a quick reversal came pushing past $90 and rose to $97.70.

However, this jump was not stable and as a result in November it has seen a big decline that has even breached $76 support and could crash to $63 or $52.

Chainstack says AI-powered security breach exposed customer data; threat now contained

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The blockchain infrastructure provider said it is treating node credentials as exposed and asking customers to rotate them and change passwords.

Blockchain infrastructure provider Chainstack revealed it experienced an AI-powered security breach in late October that exposed customer names, emails, and password hashes.

In an updated blog post on Tuesday, Chainstack said the incident has been contained and that customer-facing services continued to operate normally throughout the breach.

The company noted that an attacker first gained access to a development environment on Oct. 26, and reached parts of the production environment on Oct. 29. The firm then detected the intrusion and cut the access paths on Oct. 31. It later finalized the investigation on Nov. 2.

According to the post, the attacker accessed the system via an internet-exposed Grafana instance in a development cluster that was deployed for testing with its default administrator credentials and without an additional layer of protection.

The attacker then obtained machine credentials that reached production, and used them to access the database network. Chainstack said the attacker used the ARTEX AI agent framework to carry out the breach.

The Block has reached out to Chainstack for further comment.

Following the incident, Chainstack said it is currently treating node credentials as exposed and notifying customers to rotate them and change passwords. The company added that customer nodes, console, and API were not modified. Self-service rotation for node credentials is currently available in the console without downtime, per the post.

Chainstack also warned customers to watch for phishing attempts. "Chainstack will never ask for passwords, tokens, or keys," the team said.

The incident comes amid a wave of cyberattacks and security breaches as AI tools become increasingly capable. Earlier this week, crosschain interoperability protocol Across suffered a domain takeover, and later restored control of its website and resumed normal operations.

Why Is Crypto Crashing? Kevin O’Leary and Raoul Pal Reveal Major Reasons

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Zafar is a seasoned crypto and blockchain news writer with four years of experience. Known for accuracy, in-depth analysis, and a clear, engaging style, Zafar actively participates in blockchain communities. Beyond writing, Zafar enjoys trading and exploring the latest trends in the crypto market.

Zafar is a seasoned crypto and blockchain news writer with four years of experience. Known for accuracy, in-depth analysis, and a clear, engaging style, Zafar actively participates in blockchain communities. Beyond writing, Zafar enjoys trading and exploring the latest trends in the crypto market.

Zafar is a seasoned crypto and blockchain news writer with four years of experience. Known for accuracy, in-depth analysis, and a clear, engaging style, Zafar actively participates in blockchain communities. Beyond writing, Zafar enjoys trading and exploring the latest trends in the crypto market.

The crypto market is struggling to regain momentum as Bitcoin and other digital assets face continued pressure. Slowing US liquidity and regulatory uncertainty are two major concerns weighing on the market, according to Real Vision founder Raoul Pal and investor Kevin O’Leary.

While Pal believes tighter financial conditions are limiting money flowing into crypto, O’Leary says the lack of clear US regulations is preventing businesses from adopting blockchain technology on a wider scale.

Raoul Pal believes slowing US liquidity is one of the main reasons behind the crypto market’s weak performance.

Pal said US liquidity has remained largely flat since July 2026. A stronger dollar, rising interest rates and a flatter yield curve have slowed money creation through the banking system.

He explained that crypto needs increasing liquidity to support price growth, but current conditions are not providing enough support.

“Markets have gone nowhere. Narrow liquidity, so this is total liquidity including the banking system loans and leases. It’s not growing as fast,” Pal said.

Despite the current market downturn, he is continuing to buy crypto. He revealed that he made two purchases in 2026, including one during the February sell-off.

He believes Bitcoin is still undervalued based on its long-term liquidity trend, but does not expect an immediate recovery. He expects the US dollar to weaken towards 93 on the dollar index. Lower interest rates and a weaker dollar could improve market conditions and support crypto prices.

Pal also remains positive about Ethereum and Solana. He outlined a long-term scenario in which Ethereum could reach $18,000 by Q2 2027 and $60,000 by mid-2028 if its trend continues. However, he described these levels as a possible market path, not guaranteed targets, and identified $1,500 as an important risk level.

Kevin O’Leary points to regulatory uncertainty in the US as another reason the broader crypto market is struggling.

He believes businesses are hesitant to adopt blockchain services and stablecoins because they lack clear regulatory guidelines. The delay in passing the CLARITY Act is adding to this uncertainty.

“We really need to get that done,” O’Leary said, referring to the CLARITY Act.

O’Leary explained that his holding companies already use USDC for treasury management and cross-border payment settlements. However, many other businesses are waiting for approval from their compliance departments before adopting similar services.

He noted that the GENIUS Act has established a legal framework for stablecoins in the US, but broader rules covering blockchain infrastructure and other crypto services are still missing.

“The rest of the infrastructure and blockchain and everything else in treasury management isn’t done till the compliance department of institutions say you can do it,” he said.

According to O’Leary, this regulatory uncertainty is slowing institutional adoption and limiting the wider use of blockchain technology.

Resident Evil 1 Remake Gets Official Update From Capcom

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Capcom has officially provided an update regarding the remake of the first Resident Evil game. The developer/publisher has been steadily remaking the classics into modernized versions, more similar to what the franchise looks like today. That started back in 2019, with the release of Resident Evil 2 Remake, but we’re now looking at a new Resident Evil Remake collection for 2027. The company recently announced that it is also working on the remake of one of the most impactful entries in the series, but until now, talks about a remake of Resident Evil 1 hadn’t occurred.

As part of a special Nikkei Entertainment issue focused mainly on the 30th anniversary of Resident Evil, according to a report by Games Radar, series producer Jun Takeuchi provided some information about the remake of Resident Evil 1. The developer did not confirm or deny the long-standing rumors about the remake, but he did explain why Capcom did not choose the first entry in the series as the first remake the series would handle, and it has everything to do with how different the first game is from the second and all other installments later on.

According to Takeuchi, Resident Evil 1 wasn’t chosen as the first option for the remakes at Capcom “because it’s such a stoic game.” The producer details that the first entry in the franchise has its horror fueled by that same “stoicism,” and that the company was concerned that a remake of that game would “not resonate with today’s game fans.” The rumored remake of Resident Evil 1 would be, indeed, drastically different from Resident Evil 2 Remake, on all fronts.

What Takeuchi seems to describe is how much the first game’s horror is built around a continuously tense atmosphere and ambiance. The sequel’s horror comes mostly from a large stalking presence. Moreover, the series sees a huge pivot. While the first game is about surviving without getting caught, the franchise has constantly evolved into something a lot more action-oriented, where players have tons of different tools at their disposal to deal with the infected. Those who played the latest core Resident Evil games would see something completely different in a potential Resident Evil 1 Remake.

If a fan’s first contact with Resident Evil came with Resident Evil 5, Resident Evil 6, or Resident Evil 7: Biohazard, they’d likely expect a bit more adrenaline and action, and Resident Evil 1 is more about getting haunted by your surroundings. This isn’t a confirmation that the first entry won’t get a remake, but an explanation as to why it wasn’t Capcom’s starting point. The rumors about a Resident Evil 1 Remake are strong, but there is still something officially announced before that: the upcoming Resident Evil: Code Veronica Remake.

Given how successful the remake installments have proven to be, despite the well-deserved criticisms of Resident Evil 3 Remake, it wouldn’t be a surprise if Capcom were to continue making remakes of the series. After the Code Veronica remake, the most logical step would be to remake the oldest entry in the Resident Evil series, and if the rumors are to be believed, it’s already an unannounced reality we just have to wait for.

Is SpaceX Stock a Buy Before Its First Try at Catching a Starship Upper Stage?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SpaceX might catch both halves of Starship for the first time by Saturday. How much would that change for a $1.9 trillion stock?

SpaceX (SPCX -2.33%) wants to get its whole rocket home. On Starship's 15th test flight, set for as early as Friday, Nov. 13, the company plans to catch both halves of the rocket using its launch tower. If both catches work, SpaceX says it'd be the first full recovery of an orbital launch system.

The bottom half, the Super Heavy booster, has been caught on three past test flights. The top half hasn't. That part's the upper stage (the section that takes satellites the rest of the way to orbit, which SpaceX simply calls the ship).

Since its IPO, SpaceX's stock has traded as high as $225.64. Shares trade around $143 as I write, about 37% under that high. With a potentially historic flight a day away, is the stock a buy?

I think a successful catch would be a big milestone for Starship. But I doubt it would change much about what investors are paying for now.

SpaceX's mission page shows a 75-minute launch window starting at 4:45 p.m. Central time on Friday, pending regulatory approval as I write. The booster is due to be caught around six and a half minutes after liftoff. The ship, meanwhile, is meant to deploy 26 of Starlink's bigger V3 satellites and spend over 22 hours in orbit before trying to land at the tower in Texas. If Starship takes off on Friday, that would put the ship's catch on Saturday afternoon.

Even that final step isn't a given. SpaceX said the ship will only come back to the launch site if its flight control team confirms that the systems it needs for reentry and landing are healthy. Otherwise, it can be brought down over the ocean instead.

"Success is not guaranteed on these first flights, but I'd say it's more likely than not that this succeeds next flight," CEO Elon Musk said on the Oct. 20 earnings call.

A rocket that returns can fly again. On the same call, President Gwynne Shotwell said SpaceX's aim is to "refly the second stage by early next year." Reusing the ship is what's meant to change Starship from an expensive test program into a far cheaper way to reach orbit.

For now, the program is still getting more expensive. Showing how hard SpaceX is pushing, the space segment's research and development spending grew 44% year over year to $1.2 billion in the third quarter. According to SpaceX's quarterly filing, that spending mostly goes to developing, building, and testing Starship. And it's risen every quarter this year, from $930 million in the year's first quarter to around $1.1 billion in the June quarter.

Still, the segment nearly broke even. More customer launches helped drive space revenue up 84% sequentially to $1.8 billion, and the segment's operating loss shrank to $7 million from $542 million in the second quarter.

But a year before the same segment turned a $259 million operating profit.

The bigger payoff from a reusable ship could arguably show up in Starlink. SpaceX's own payloads made up 358 of the 476 metric tons it sent to orbit in the third quarter. Plus, SpaceX says the 26 V3 satellites on Starship's first orbital flight added around 10 times the downlink capacity of a Falcon 9 Starlink launch.

SpaceX's valuation is around $1.9 trillion at the current price. Even annualizing its third-quarter revenue of $14.6 billion, the shares trade at around 33 times sales.

And most of that revenue has little to do with Starship now. The super intelligence segment, SpaceX's artificial intelligence (AI) business, pulled in $7.6 billion last quarter, around half the total. Connectivity, home to Starlink, added $5.2 billion. The space segment's $1.8 billion was around 12%.

So, a catch would likely help the long-term case more than the next few quarters. Starship still needs to fly again, fly often (Musk said on the call he expects weekly flights sometime in the first half of next year), and show lower costs in the numbers.

Sure, a miss or a delay probably wouldn't hurt today's business much. After all, Falcon 9 already launches Starlink satellites. A setback could mainly cost time and more quarters of growing Starship spending.

Is SpaceX stock a buy ahead of Friday's launch? Not for a tower landing alone, in my view. At around 33 times sales, the growth stock's price depends mainly on AI computing and Starlink, and the Starship tab is still rising.

I wouldn't buy shares just ahead of the flight.

Custom Ad Algorithms Are Helping Schibsted Media Win Budgets Back From Meta

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For years, ad budgets have been shifting from the open web and independent publishers to walled garden platforms like Meta.

But recently, that shift has begun to reverse a bit.

Nearly two thirds – 63.9% – of advertisers have moved mobile budgets from walled gardens to independent partners in the past year, according to a July survey of 166 brands and agencies by eMarketer and Datum Intelligence. Only 1.2% of the survey respondents shifted their budgets the opposite way.

That being said, eMarketer projects Meta, Google and Amazon will attract 62.3% of worldwide digital ad spend this year, with Meta topping Google in ad revenue for the first time.

Still, publishers around the world, including Norwegian-based broadcaster and TV news conglomerate Schibsted Media Group, are trying to take advantage of any shift away from Meta and the other walled gardens they can get.

Schibsted is working with Nodals AI, a London-based company that creates custom ad optimization algorithms for publishers in Europe and the US, to help court brands who’ve been wooed by Meta’s own optimization tools, Christer Ljones, director of ad data capabilities at Schibsted Marketing Services, told AdExchanger.

Schibsted owns VG, the largest digital news publisher in Norway, boasting about 2.4 million daily visitors. It also owns Aftonbladet, one of the largest digital news publishers in Sweden, which claims 3.6 million daily visitors. And it acquired Sweden’s TV4 and Finland’s MTV Oy – two of the largest TV broadcasters in those countries – from Swedish telecommunications group Telia last year.

These properties, combined with the rest of Schibsted’s local newspaper network, gives the company roughly 50% audience reach in the geos it covers, Ljones said.

To put Schibsted’s audience size in perspective compared to US publishers, its largest properties, VG and Aftonbladet, have wider reach than the Los Angeles Times, which attracts a combined weekly online and print audience of 4.4 million, or about 11% of California’s population.

VG and Aftonbladet make most of their money from advertising, with the local publications more reliant on subscriptions, he said. Online display ads and branded content account for most of the company’s ad revenue, with a roughly even split between the two.

Like other large publishers with strong reach concentrated in specific markets, Schibsted has long pitched advertisers on working with the company directly to take advantage of its market saturation and its deep knowledge of its audiences, Ljones said. The direct ad business comprises roughly two thirds of Schibsted’s ad revenue, with programmatic making up the remaining third.

But audience reach isn’t the only thing that matters to performance-minded brands. The walled gardens have won advertisers over with AI-powered campaign optimization features like Meta’s Advantage+, Ljones said, and it’s been hard for open-web publishers to keep up on the ad tech side.

It’s also been harder for open web publishers to compete when it comes to direct sales, said Nodals Founder and CEO Aly Nurmohamed.

“Advertisers have been trained by Meta to just care about ‘I put money in and I see how much money comes out,’” Nurmohamed said. “So the problem for Schibsted was advertisers were saying, ‘I don’t want to buy on a CPM, and you’re really expensive relative to Meta. Here’s what I’m doing with Meta. Can you replicate it?’”

Schibsted has always seen Meta as a competitor and not a “partner,” Ljones said. While some publishers experienced short-term gains from working with Meta in the past, he added, the platform is no longer shy about cutting publishers out of its ad business.

That dynamic is part of the reason Schibsted has been leading the charge against Meta’s planned terms of service update. The update would have required all publishers active on Meta platforms to share data for training the company’s AI models – essentially, training the optimization tools that are helping Meta take publishers’ portion of ad revenue.

Schibsted, along with 180 other publishers, joined the Norwegian Media Businesses’ Association in suing Meta over its TOS update at the end of October. Within two days of the lawsuit being filed, the Oslo District Court ruled in the publishers’ favor, imposing a freeze on Meta’s TOS update in Norway until its legality could be investigated. Legal decisions like this one likely prompted Meta to pause its TOS update across all regions earlier this month.

But legal battles are just one way Schibsted is fighting against Meta’s market dominance. Custom algorithms, like those Nodals creates, are another weapon in its arsenal.

Schibsted sees these custom algorithms as a way to remind advertisers of its effectiveness in driving performance and brand awareness by using similar tools to those offered by Meta, Ljones said.

Nodals pitches itself as a solution for publishers to create their own Meta-esque optimization engines.

And Schibsted could be in a strong position to win back some of the business it’s lost to Meta. It’s maintained a direct relationship with its audience through its owned and operated properties, even as other publishers struggle with AI search stealing their traffic, according to Ljones. As a result, it’s only reliant on search for a single-digit percentage of the traffic on its largest sites.

That direct relationship with its audiences gives Schibsted the first-party data insights it needs to compete with Meta’s campaign optimization capabilities, Nurmohamed said.

“We take their data and feed it into their own models, collecting everything they know about what their audiences are, any logged-in data they have, how far you scroll down the page, how often you click on content or ads,” he said. “All of that goes into our model, and then you can have a powerful prediction on who is likely to click and buy on a particular advertiser.”

Plus, because the publisher owns the optimization algorithm and the data that powers it, Nodals’ solutions shouldn’t be affected by Apple’s recent efforts to crack down on third-party audience data vendors and ID graphs, Nurmohamed said.

Schibsted first tested Nodal’s tech last summer and has been incorporating it into its direct sales efforts since those successful tests, Ljones said. It’s been building a new product line of direct-sold native ad formats with AI optimization built in, priced on a cost-per-click basis, rather than on a CPM model.

The new products are resonating with some of Schibsted’s traditional advertisers, such as grocery stores serving the Norway and Sweden markets, who are looking for a mix of performance and brand awareness from their ad campaigns, he said. And, while Schibsted “won’t be necessarily as good as Meta on average” when it comes to driving performance, “you don’t need to beat them on the average. You just need to beat them with meaningful scale at the margin.”

For example, Schibsted has found success pitching advertisers that have been pouring more money into Meta lately but seeing diminishing returns, Ljones said.

“We get feedback from advertisers that they’ve seen their Meta ROI, their conversions, flatten out, or maybe even decline slightly,” he said. “So, the overall idea that we see initially working is, ‘Let’s take 10% of whatever you spend on Meta, and you can probably keep most of your conversions and get more [from us].”

Ljones feels confident that Schibsted can build on those margins going forward.

“We’re in an early phase, but we’re seeing the right results,” he said. “For us, that’s taking new budgets from existing clients, defending existing budgets in a few cases, but also addressing clients we basically lost.”

AI is taking over the media and tech industries in the name of simplification, and marketers are pining for provable campaign outcomes, including from TV. These shifts are also causing significant change in Agency Holdco Land, according to Angela Steele, the first chief client officer for WPP Media US.

No major retail media player has taken as circumspect an approach to ad revenue as Shopify. But the company has recently taken its next important step with a product called Campaign Autopilot.

OpenAI’s head of monetization, Benji Shomair makes the case for ads as a way to bring ChatGPT to more people, with user trust taking priority over advertiser demands.

In Q3, The Trade Desk beat the admittedly pessimistic revenue forecast it set for itself earlier this year. But growth has stalled.

Viant is doubling down on CTV with its $20 million Waymark acquisition, betting that AI-generated ads can bring more advertisers to streaming TV.

TIME is using AI agents to draft proposals and troubleshoot campaigns – but not to replace the human relationships that drive ad sales.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

One of the perks of the streaming era is that, unlike the 20-episode seasons of network TV yore, television shows nowadays are in that sweet spot of 6-10 episodes, meaning that you can easily crush an entire season of great TV in a single weekend.

Apple TV certainly is no exception — the streaming service is home to plenty of quick comedies, high-tension limited series and short-story anthologies that are seemingly made to finish before Monday rolls around yet again.

And these three in particular are exceptionally star-studded affairs, despite their comparatively short episode orders, led by A-listers like Jake Gyllenhaal, Matthew McConaughey, Taron Egerton, and Woody Harrelson. Whether you're after a breezy buddy comedy or a slow-burn psychological thriller, here are three Apple TV shows you can binge-watch in one weekend.

Based on the 2010 autobiographical book "In with the Devil" by James Keene and Hillel Levin, this true-crime drama centers on Jimmy Keene, a high school football hero and decorated policeman's son, whose clean-cut life is upended when he is sentenced to 10 years in a minimum-security prison for dealing narcotics.

That all changes when he's given a high-stakes deal with the FBI: Enter a maximum-security prison and befriend suspected serial killer Larry Hall (Paul Walter Hauser, who won an Emmy and Golden Globe award for his chilling work here) to extract a confession, or serve his full sentence with no possibility for parole.

Across six gripping episodes, the moody psychological thriller thrums with unsettling tension and moral complexity. An ensemble cast that includes Sepideh Moafi, Greg Kinnear and the late, great Ray Liotta doesn't hurt, either.

Tesla’s Cheapest Cybertruck Ever Is An RC Racer That Goes 37 MPH

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

The Tesla Cybertruck was never as affordable as Elon Musk envisioned back in 2019, when the angular electric pickup truck was unveiled as a concept. Now, though, the American EV maker is selling a sub-$1,000 miniature version of the controversial model, right on the verge of its discontinuation.

The Tesla Cybertruck RC Racer was added to the company’s web shop today, as spotted by Tesla enthusiast Sawyer Merritt on X. The 1:10-scale remote-controlled EV features all-wheel drive, a rechargeable lithium-polymer battery, and independent suspension with plenty of travel.

The company claims its RC Racer’s 2.4-gigaherz radio system can maintain high-speed stability at distances of up to 326 feet (100 meters), while an optional Gyro feature adds counter-steering to help prevent spinouts.

A single brushless motor sends power to both axles through a set of drive shafts, enabling a top speed of 37 miles per hour (60 kilometers per hour). That’s very impressive, and it makes the small-scale Cybertruck faster than all low-speed vehicles (LSVs) in the United States, which are capped at just 25 mph.

Tesla bundles a USB-C charger in the box, which is a welcome addition in a world where smartphone makers are no longer including charging cables, let alone charging bricks. The Cybertruck RC Racer has an advertised run time of up to 20 minutes, and judging from the promotional video posted on the company’s website, it looks like a ton of fun.

To charge the battery, the polycarbonate body needs to be removed from the chassis via the quick-release clips. Then, the battery needs to be disconnected from the motor and connected to the on-board charger, and charging can begin via the USB-C port on the side of the chassis. According to Tesla, a full charge may take up to three hours, which can be a bit of a buzzkill, but the company said it will soon start selling spare parts for the RC Racer, so buyers will probably be able to get a spare battery or two to keep the fun going for longer.

The included transmitter has a side wheel for steering and a trigger for acceleration, braking, and reverse. Separate controls for the throttle trim, steering alignment trim, steering reverse, and gyro stability assist are also included. The transmitter is powered by four AA batteries, which are not included in the box.

Tesla’s Cybertryck RC Racer is available now for $350 on the company’s online shop, with the first shipments expected to arrive at customers’ doors starting next week. According to the company, the RC car is not suitable for children under 14 years old.
