‘I Play Rocky’ Opens 50th Anniversary Edition of Cairo Film Festival as Chief Hussein Fahmy Looks to the Future

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As the Cairo Intl. Film Festival celebrates 50 years since its founding, president Hussein Fahmy wants younger Egyptians to rediscover their country’s rich cinematic heritage. For the veteran actor, introducing new audiences to the films that established Egypt as the Arab world’s cinematic capital is central to the festival’s future.

Speaking to Variety ahead of the 47th edition, which runs Nov. 11–20, Fahmy emphasized the importance of preserving that legacy. “This remains my dream, to have this young generation aware of our history, of how Egyptian cinema was actually dominating the whole Middle East.” He sees the festival as an important platform for bringing that history to new audiences, particularly through screenings of restored Egyptian classics. Related Stories ‘You Can See Everything’ Ruled Ineligible for DGA Feature Prize, Will Compete Only in Documentary Category Latvian Oscar Submission ‘Ulya’ Sells Wide Ahead of Opening Tallinn: ‘This Film Is a Blockbuster’ (EXCLUSIVE)

Fahmy also wants Cairo to stand alongside the world’s major film festivals, an ambition he believes depends on building audiences’ confidence in its programming. “The more credibility the Cairo Film Festival has for the spectator, the more they trust us with the movies that we bring, with the jury that we select,” he said.

Cairo’s credibility was shaken somewhat following news that one of the films in Critics’ Week competition, “Dancing Angels,” had been pulled from the lineup, before the invitation was eventually restored, although the filmmaking team then declined the opportunity to premiere the film there. (See full story here.)

But attracting audiences, Fahmy acknowledges, is an increasingly unpredictable undertaking. “The situation, whether it be the social situation, whether it be the international situation, is affecting the taste of the spectator. It’s affecting the mood of the spectator,” he said.

This year’s edition brings together 145 films from 64 countries, including 17 world premieres, five international premieres and 87 Middle East and North Africa premieres. The lineup spans acclaimed international directors, emerging Arab filmmakers and restored Egyptian classics, alongside new programs aimed at younger audiences.

The festival opens with “I Play Rocky,” directed by Peter Farrelly (“Green Book,” 2019 Academy Awards best picture winner), which chronicles Sylvester Stallone’s struggle to bring his screenplay for “Rocky” to the screen while insisting on playing the lead role himself. The selection carries particular significance for the anniversary edition as the original “Rocky” was released in 1976, the same year CIFF was founded.

The international competition includes two Egyptian productions: Ashgan El-Hamus’ “Al Baraneya,” about a family living among jasmine fields outside Cairo whose livelihood is threatened by a landowner’s persistent offer to purchase their property, and the world premiere of Ahmad Abdalla’s “BerleeN_,” which follows three Arab strangers confronting their pasts over the course of a day in the German capital. The feature is produced by Film Clinic, the Cairo-based production outfit headed by veteran producer and former CIFF director Mohamed Hefzy.

Other regional contenders include Lebanese filmmaker Nadim Tabet’s “In the Darkness I See You,” a supernatural drama set around a construction site where tensions rise between Syrian workers and Lebanese villagers, and Moroccan director Laïla Marrakchi’s “Strawberries,” which follows Moroccan seasonal workers facing exploitation on farms in southern Spain. The latter premiered in Cannes’ Un Certain Regard section and is Morocco’s submission for the international feature Oscar.

The 12-film competition also features “Heysel 85,” from Romanian-Belgian director Teodora Ana Mihai, which revisits the deadly 1985 football stadium disaster in Brussels that left 39 people dead; Austrian director Adrian Goiginger’s “Four Minus Three,” about a professional clown coping with the loss of her family; and Iranian filmmaker Morteza Alizadeh’s “Inherited,” which follows a woman on death row who learns that pregnancy is her only way to delay her execution. “Inherited” will have its world premiere in Cairo.

Moroccan filmmaker Nabil Ayouch heads the international competition jury, whose members include Chinese actor Fan Bingbing and Belgian director Laura Wandel.

The Horizons of Arab Cinema competition includes seven films, among them the world premiere of Egyptian director Sara Shazli’s documentary “Your Daughter.” Shazli explores her relationship with her mother, filmmaker and producer Marianne Khoury, niece of legendary Egyptian director Youssef Chahine. As she prepares to become a mother herself, Shazli reflects on how Khoury’s lifelong devotion to cinema shaped her childhood.

Also making its world premiere is Iraqi director Ali Toffan Al-Fatlawi’s “Al Baseer – The Blind Ferryman,” which follows a blind ferryman living in the marshes of southern Iraq who falls in love with a mysterious woman whose existence no one else believes.

Other Horizons selections include Algerian filmmaker Mia Bendrimia’s documentary “Magma,” which delves into the effects of colonialism on her family across Algeria and France, and Lebanese director Rania Rafei’s “The Day of Wrath: Tales from Tripoli,” tracing decades of political and social upheaval in the northern Lebanese city.

The out-of-competition lineup features internationally acclaimed films, including Andrey Zvyagintsev’s Cannes Grand Prix winner “Minotaur,” Emin Alper’s Berlin Silver Bear winner “Salvation” and Valeska Grisebach’s Cannes Jury Prize winner “The Dreamed Adventure.” Japanese filmmaker Hirokazu Kore-eda is represented by two films, “Look Back” and 2026 Palme d’Or nominee “Sheep in the Box,” while other selections include Radu Jude’s “The Diary of a Chambermaid,” Mahamat-Saleh Haroun’s “Soumsoum, the Night of the Stars” and Rodrigo Sorogoyen’s “The Beloved.”

The festival’s commitment to Egyptian film history is particularly visible in its Restored Egyptian Classics program, launched last year and featuring 27 titles in this edition. Among them are Salah Abu Seif’s “Cairo 30” and “The Second Wife,” Henry Barakat’s “The Sin,” Youssef Chahine’s “The People and the Nile” and Hassan el-Imam’s adaptations of Naguib Mahfouz’s “Palace Walk” and “Palace of Desire.”

“I find that the festival is a very good platform to screen all the restored movies of Egyptian cinema, of its black and white cinema,” Fahmy emphasized. “It’s a wonderful history and I would love everyone to be aware of it as they go into the future.”

The anniversary edition will also honor filmmakers Matteo Garrone and Abderrahmane Sissako with Golden Pyramid Honorary Awards for Lifetime Achievement, alongside Egyptian screenwriter Medhat El Adl and cinematographer Saeed Shimi. Egyptian actor Asser Yassin will receive the Faten Hamama Award for Excellence.

The festival is also expanding its efforts to reach younger audiences through its New Wave program, a new Cairo Kids section and an extended reality program. Its industry arm, Cairo Industry Days, running Nov. 14–19, will host masterclasses and conversations with filmmakers and industry figures, alongside financing, training and networking opportunities, particularly for filmmakers from the Arab world.

For Fahmy, the festival’s future ultimately depends on building on Egypt’s cinematic legacy while attracting a new generation of filmgoers. “As the festival gets older, I want it to become younger,” Fahmy reflected.

Crypto Market Sell-Off Deepens: Why Are Bitcoin, Ethereum and Solana Falling?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Shubham Vishwakarma is a crypto market analyst and technical content writer who covers price action, on-chain signals, and breaking blockchain news. He simplifies complex market data into sharp, easy-to-understand insights, helping readers stay ahead of trends in Bitcoin, altcoins, and DeFi. His writing combines technical precision with compelling market storytelling.

Bitcoin, Ethereum and Solana extended losses as selling pressure spread across the broader crypto market.

More than $1 billion in leveraged positions were liquidated, accelerating the decline.

Persistent ETF outflows and uncertainty surrounding US monetary policy are weakening institutional demand.

The cryptocurrency market is struggling to find support as Bitcoin, Ethereum and major altcoins extend their declines. Bitcoin traded near $73,400 on November 11, while Ethereum slipped toward $2,050 and Solana fell below $85. The broader market has come under pressure from institutional withdrawals, leveraged liquidations and growing macroeconomic uncertainty.

With several major cryptocurrencies approaching important support levels, investors are increasingly questioning whether the latest correction has further room to run. Is the crypto market approaching a bottom, or could another wave of selling push prices lower?

The sell-off has extended beyond Bitcoin, with major altcoins recording steeper losses as investors reduce exposure to riskier digital assets. Bitcoin has struggled to recover above $75,000 after falling to its lowest levels since August. Ethereum has faced heavier selling, dropping toward $2,050, while Solana has slipped below $85.

The pressure is also visible across the wider market. XRP and other large-cap altcoins also faced significant selling pressure, highlighting the breadth of the correction.

Leveraged trading has amplified the market decline. Crypto futures liquidations reached approximately $1.1 billion in the 24 hours reported on November 9, with long positions accounting for a substantial share.

Ethereum recorded particularly heavy liquidations, reflecting the pressure on traders who had positioned for a price recovery. Meanwhile, futures trading volume surged above $180 billion as open interest declined to approximately $127 billion.

This combination points to significant position unwinding. As leveraged bullish bets are closed automatically, additional selling can push prices lower and trigger further liquidations, creating a cycle of downward pressure.

Institutional demand has also weakened as investors withdraw capital from spot crypto investment products. US spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded two consecutive weeks of net outflows, while Ethereum and Solana investment products also experienced withdrawals. Earlier in November, combined outflows across major US crypto ETFs exceeded $1.1 billion in a single week.

These withdrawals have become an important headwind because ETF demand has been a significant source of institutional buying. Continued redemptions could make it harder for Bitcoin and other major cryptocurrencies to establish a sustained recovery.

The broader economic environment is adding another layer of pressure. Stronger-than-expected US employment data and renewed expectations of tighter Federal Reserve policy have reduced appetite for speculative assets.

Political uncertainty following the US midterm elections has also raised questions about the future direction of cryptocurrency regulation. Meanwhile, concerns surrounding the reported $6 billion Binance security breach have added to industry-wide uncertainty. Together, these developments have weakened investor confidence, leaving digital assets vulnerable to further volatility.

The immediate outlook depends on whether Bitcoin can regain support and whether ETF flows begin to stabilize. Ethereum and Solana also need to attract fresh buying interest to prevent the correction from spreading further across altcoins

Better Tech ETF for 2027: State Street's XLK vs. Fidelity's FTEC

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Both funds charge just 0.08%, but their holdings counts differ sharply, and long-term returns reveal a narrow gap.

The State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK +3.23%) and Fidelity MSCI Information Technology Index ETF (FTEC +2.91%) both provide ultra-low-cost technology exposure, differing primarily in their breadth of holdings and long-term risk-adjusted returns.

Selecting a technology fund often comes down to a choice between large-cap concentration and total-market breadth. While both funds are anchored by the same massive software and hardware leaders, their portfolio construction creates different outcomes for investors looking for mid-cap participation within the tech sector.

Beta measures price volatility relative to the S&P 500; beta is calculated from monthly returns over the available fund history (up to five years). The 1-year return represents total return over the trailing 12 months. Dividend yield is the trailing-12-month distribution yield.

Both funds carry an identical expense ratio of 0.08%, making them two of the most affordable options in the category. The yield difference is minimal, with the State Street fund providing a slightly higher payout than its Fidelity peer.

The State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF focuses on the technology portion of the S&P 500, offering exposure to the largest and most established companies in the industry. The portfolio is strictly 100% tech and holds 76 stocks, meaning it skips over the small-cap segment to favor stability and size. Its largest positions include Apple (AAPL +2.04%) at 16.41%, Nvidia (NVDA +2.87%) at 13.19%, and Microsoft (MSFT -2.26%) at 12.77%. The fund was launched in 1998, and has paid $0.83 per share over the trailing 12 months, which on its recent ~$168.9 share price works out to a 0.5% yield.

The Fidelity MSCI Information Technology Index ETF tracks a much broader index, including 294 holdings that reach further down the market-capitalization scale. Its sector makeup is 99% technology, with trace amounts in industrials and financial services. Its largest positions include Apple at 18.96%, Nvidia at 14.80%, and Microsoft at 13.21%. This fund was launched in 2013 and manages $20.8 billion in assets under management (AUM), while paying $1.11 per share over the trailing 12 months, which on its recent ~$263.8 share price works out to a 0.4% yield.

For more guidance on ETF investing, check out the full guide at this link.

Investors seeking exposure to the hot technology industry have two distinct choices in the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK) and the Fidelity MSCI Information Technology Index ETF (FTEC). Both share similarities, such as their expense ratios, betas, and dividend yields.

The key factors setting XLK and FTEC apart are their AUMs and the size of their holdings. XLK's far larger AUM gives it greater liquidity, making it an ideal fund for active traders. It also focuses only on companies within the S&P 500, granting you exposure to the biggest names in tech. However, this concentration means you miss out on the growth potential of smaller companies.

FTEC's key strength is its superior diversification compared to XLK. With nearly 300 holdings, this ETF provides broader exposure across the technology sector. This also helps to cushion against a single stock impacting overall performance.

Whether FTEC or XLK is right for you depends on your goals. If you want greater liquidity and a focus on the big tech companies, XLK is the better choice. If you seek broad exposure to tech businesses to capture the greater upside potential of mid- and small-cap stocks, FTEC is the way to go.

Meccha Chameleon’s 4 Golden Joystick Wins Set New Indie Record

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

The Golden Joystick Awards have been running for 44 years now. That's a staggering amount of time, especially since video games haven't been commercially available for much longer than that. It also means many of the ceremony's records are pretty well established.

During last night's show, however, one two-person indie shattered all expectations, scooping four awards and breaking one of those 44-year-old records in the process.

Meccha Chameleon was developed by two people: Lemorion and Haganeiro. It took roughly five months to develop, and it quickly became one of 2026's biggest hits, selling over 20 million copies (more than Pokemon Legends Z-A, God of War Ragnarok, and Resident Evil Village's lifetime sales).

Last night, it scooped four awards at the Golden Joysticks, sharing the most wins on the night with eventual Game of the Year winner Resident Evil Requiem. It topped every category it was nominated in, except Best PC Game, which was won by Crimson Desert, and those four wins meant it took home more awards at a single Golden Joysticks ceremony than any other indie game.

It beat Marathon, Valheim, and Arc Raiders to win Best Multiplayer; Mewgenics and Cairn to win Best Self-Published Indie; and Mortal Shell 2 and Orbitals to win Developers' Choice. It also won the Streamers' Choice award.

The previous record for most wins by an indie in a single night was shared by multiple games, at 2. That list contains some behemoths, including Hollow Knight: Silksong, Balatro, Cult of the Lamb, Hades, and Outer Wilds.

If you include Baldur's Gate 3 and Clair Obscur: Expedition 33 as indie, then Meccha Chameleon didn't quite grab the record, as both won seven in a single night; however, because their status is disputed, many won't include them.

Meccha Chameleon wasn't the only record breaker at last night's Golden Joysticks. With its win in the Best Remake/Remaster category, The Witcher 3 became the game with the most outright wins in the ceremony's history, with 10.

Capcom also had a stellar evening. Three of its games won awards: Pragmata for Best Supporting Actor, Onimusha: Way of the Sword for Best Sound Design, and Resident Evil Requiem picked up four wins, netting the show's overall Game of the Year for the studio. It also won Studio of the Year, beating Nintendo, HouseHouse, and Square Enix, winning that award with the highest vote percentage of the entire evening (57%). You can check out a breakdown of every winner right here.

xStocks owns 75% of Solana’s tokenized stocks – Rises to $607M in a year

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

xStocks has started listing stocks from outside the US! They dropped 200+ UK and European stocks on Solana [SOL] this week.

xStocks owns most of Solana’s tokenized stock market

But why is this big news? Per data from Blockworks, the supply of tokenized equities on Solana is at $607 million. They report 2,102 products and close to 1.89 million holders.

Back in June 2025, that number was basically zilch. At the beginning of the year, we were at $200 million; $400 million around April. Sometime this fall, we hit $600 million!

The October peak was $620 million, but we’ve fallen down since. Honestly, though? It’s mostly just kept going up.

Most of that has to do with xStocks; their tokens are 75% of the supply. Ondo, Backpack, and Superstate each have a single-digit share; smaller issuers such as PreStocks, Tessera Labs, Remora, and Securitize put up whatever’s left.

Those shares haven’t moved an inch since August, other than a short dip for xStocks a few days ago. There’s a ton of competition to put traditional stocks on Solana, but it’s xStocks’ race to lose.

But remember: Holders are counted by address, and some people use more than one. The real number of investors could be lower.

Blockworks showed about 400,000 holder addresses in early September. Today, it’s closer to 1.9 million.

Daily senders’ best day was at 320K, and the count has gone past 200K on several other days since.

Avalanche is set to settle traders for the NYSE-linked tokenized stock platform. Robinhood is building its own chain on Arbitrum. The DTCC’s tokenization service uses Canton for part of its setup, and word on the street is that Stellar is prepping for an RWA push too!

Everyone wants a piece of the Wall Street pie. Right now, that’s on Solana’s plate. It could be someone else tomorrow.

A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

Skooner turns a Kubernetes service-account token into a dashboard session

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Skooner is a Kubernetes dashboard that can read and change cluster resources. Its install guide supports service-account tokens, OIDC, and a NodePort access path. The token that a user enters can inherit substantial cluster authority. A ZoomEye title count is useful for finding possible dashboards, but the token's role bindings and lifetime are the security decision.

On 2026-11-11 at 19:48 UTC, title="Skooner" returned 1,571 ZoomEye matches with sub_type=all, page 1, and page size 1. The application field returned zero, the body field returned 1,574, and the legacy title="k8dash" candidate returned 64. The product's former name is a reminder that inventory queries need a rename check.

The search is available at ZoomEye. A title match does not prove that a cluster is reachable or that the token exposed on the login screen has cluster-admin rights.

The installation instructions describe a service account and a token secret. A user copies that token into the dashboard login. The dashboard server proxies requests to the Kubernetes API. If the service account is bound to a broad role, every session using the token inherits that authority.

Create a dedicated service account for the dashboard and bind only the roles the operators need. Prefer OIDC and short-lived credentials when the cluster supports them. Do not reuse the cluster-admin token from a bootstrap process as the daily dashboard login. Test read, list, create, and delete operations separately for each role.

The install guide offers a NodePort option for quick access. A NodePort is a network exposure decision, not just a service configuration. Restrict it with network policy, a firewall, or an authenticated proxy. If the dashboard is only for a few administrators, a private address or port-forward is safer than publishing the NodePort.

Test from an untrusted network and confirm that the login page and any API routes are not reachable. Then test from the approved administrative network. Record the ingress path, TLS termination, and authentication method so that a later service change does not silently remove them.

The dashboard can update YAML, scale workloads, and manage resources. Enable Kubernetes audit logging and review the service account and user identities that appear in the logs. Alert on token creation, role-binding changes, secret reads, and resource changes made through the dashboard namespace.

The 1,571 title matches are not a count of vulnerable clusters. They are a seed for checking token scope and network placement. Skooner is useful when its login token is a narrow, expiring identity and the dashboard is kept on an administrative path.

Google introduces Gemini Decisions API that scores 1,000 closed-set choices in a single pass

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

📅 November 11, 2026 • Google blog — Google introduced the Gemini Decisions API, a new endpoint for fast closed-set decisions, powered by a new model, Gemini Decision 1 (gemini-decision-1), via the Gemini API in Google AI Studio. Instead of generating answer text token by token, Gemini Decision 1 scores candidate options in a single forward pass and returns probabilities plus confidence scores. One request carries a shared multimodal input across text, images, audio, video and PDFs of up to 256K tokens, with up to 1,000 independent questions evaluated against it.

Three question types, one confidence score Questions come in three types: a yes/no probability called noul, multiple choice with 2 to 255 options returning normalized probabilities for every candidate, and ordered rubric scoring returning a continuous weighted score with per-level probabilities. Every answer includes a confidence score from 0 to 1, so code can route ambiguous cases to a fallback or a human reviewer. Purpose-built use cases named by Google are tool selection, policy checks, model routing and context grading. The confidence score lets code gate on certainty.

Latency and pricing Google reports end-to-end latency of 150–250 ms, median model compute of 50–100 ms contexts up to 16K tokens, with latency nearly flat from 1 to 100 questions per request. Pricing: USD 0.10 per 1M input tokens with zero output tokens billed. Per Google, this makes it nearly 5x cheaper across a shared benchmark suite than Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite with the Interactions API. A no-cost tier exists in Google AI Studio. Per Google, it leads benchmarks in routing, reasoning, ranking and multimodal understanding with the lowest calibration error.

Where Google runs it and what changes Per Google, internal uses include dynamic model routing in Gemini Notebook with up to 40 percent lower latency for daily queries, wireframing in Stitch, emoji selection in CC from more than 200 options, and a safety layer for MCP tools in Google Search. Integrations are rolling out with OpenRouter, LangChain, Vercel AI SDK, LiteLLM, Pydantic AI, Pipecat, LiveKit and OpenCode alongside the google-genai SDKs.

IMPACT — for agent builders: one-pass scoring changes the cost of routing, ranking and policy checks inside agent stacks.

Scores arrive in one response. Sources: blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/developers-tools/gemini-decisions-api x.com/sundarpichai/status/2120581155078729728 mezha.net/eng/news/mezha-c06fca3b9549fb95708df0f2_google_introduces_gemini

Stripe approved the payment. That's not the question a license-key store needs answered.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Here's a fraud scenario physical ecommerce rarely has to think about.

Someone buys a game key with a stolen card. The key is revealed instantly. Within minutes it's redeemed, or resold on a grey-market site.

Weeks later the chargeback arrives. You refund the money. You don't get the key back. No package to intercept, no return to process, no address to flag. The product left the moment the page loaded.

That's daily life for Increddy, a license-key storefront, and it's why "Stripe handles fraud" wasn't enough.

Payment processors rank digital goods among the highest-risk categories, and the reasons stack up:

Every risk decision that matters has to happen before checkout completes. Afterwards, you're just counting losses.

To be clear: Stripe's fraud tooling is good, and it stays in Increddy's stack. It scores the payment (card, device, transaction pattern) against signals from across Stripe's network.

It doesn't answer "is this payment risky **given that the product is an instantly resellable key we can never claw back?"

Those are different questions, and a key store needs both answered.

The design choice that matters isn't any single fraud rule. It's where the risk check sits.

A check that runs after Stripe approves is only reacting to a decision that's already been made. So Increddy's risk scoring is a first-class Medusa module inside the checkout flow with the authority to block checkout completion on its own, whatever Stripe returned.

It gets a vote before the order exists, not a note attached to a transaction that already happened.

You might have noticed there's no list of signals or thresholds in this post. That's deliberate.

It's the same reason banks don't publish their fraud rules: a risk system works partly because the people it targets can't read the rulebook. The architecture is worth sharing. The heuristics aren't, because publishing them would make them worse at their job.

Treat fraud risk as a property of the product category, not only the payment. If what you sell is valuable the instant it's delivered and impossible to take back, give your own risk logic a real seat at checkout, with real veto power.

The fraud layer was one of several custom modules in Increddy's move off Shopify. See the full case study on kryeo.com →

The longer version of this post is on the Kryeo blog.

We're Kryeo. We build checkout and risk logic for headless stores through our Medusa development work.

I Deployed My First Django App on Shared Hosting and Broke It 7 Times. Here's What Fixed It.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

description: The real, unfiltered story of getting Django live on cPanel/DirectAdmin-style hosting, with the exact mistakes and fixes. tags: django, python, webdev, beginners cover_image: The "It Works on My Machine" Lie My Django app ran perfectly on localhost:8000. Beautiful pages. Zero errors. I felt like a genius. Then I uploaded it to a real server. 500 Internal Server Error. No message. No hint. Just a white page staring at me like it knew what I'd done. If you've ever lived this moment, this post is for you. Here are the 7 ways I broke my deployment, in order, so you can skip straight to the fix. Break #1: Debug Mode Was Hiding the Truth On my laptop, DEBUG = True showed me friendly error pages. In production, DEBUG = False showed me nothing. The fix: Check your server's error log first, before touching any code. The answer is almost always sitting there in plain text.

DEBUG = False ALLOWED_HOSTS = ["yourdomain.com", "www.yourdomain.com"] 💡 Forgetting ALLOWED_HOSTS is the #1 reason a fresh deploy fails. Break #2: My Static Files Vanished The site loaded, but with no CSS. It looked like a 1998 homepage. Django doesn't serve static files in production the way runserver does. You have to collect them: STATIC_URL = "/static/" STATIC_ROOT = BASE_DIR / "staticfiles" python manage.py collectstatic Then point your web server at that folder. Suddenly, colors. 🎨 Break #3: Wrong Python Version My laptop ran Python 3.12. The server defaulted to 3.8. Cue a dozen cryptic syntax errors. The fix: Create the app with the right Python version in your host's "Setup Python App" panel, and always pin your versions: pip freeze > requirements.txt Break #4: Secrets in the Code (Please Don't Do This) I almost pushed my SECRET_KEY to GitHub. Almost. import os SECRET_KEY = os.environ.get("DJANGO_SECRET_KEY") Move secrets into environment variables. Your future self will thank you. So will your bank account if you ever add payment keys. Break #5: The Database That Wasn't There SQLite worked locally. On the server, I hit migration errors and file permission problems. Two lessons: Run python manage.py migrate on the server, not just locally. For anything serious, switch to MySQL or PostgreSQL and keep credentials in environment variables. Break #6: The Passenger/WSGI Confusion Shared hosts don't run runserver. They need a WSGI entry point that tells the server where your app lives. If your app won't start, check that your startup file points to the right module: from myproject.wsgi import application One typo in the project name = instant 500. Ask me how I know. Break #7: I Never Restarted the App I fixed the bug. Refreshed. Same error. I fixed it again. Same error. For an hour. The app was still running the old code. Every time you change something on the server, restart the application. It's a button. It's always been a button. My Deployment Checklist (Steal This) [ ] DEBUG = False [ ] ALLOWED_HOSTS set correctly [ ] Secrets in environment variables [ ] collectstatic run [ ] Correct Python version selected [ ] requirements.txt installed on the server [ ] Migrations applied [ ] App restarted [ ] Error logs checked first when something breaks The Real Lesson Deployment isn't a separate skill from coding. It's the moment your code meets reality. Every error I hit taught me something about how the web actually works: what a server does, where files live, why configuration matters. Breaking things 7 times made me a better developer than 7 tutorials did. So if your first deploy is on fire right now, good. You're learning.

Your Turn 👇 What's the worst deployment bug you've ever hit? Drop it in the comments. I'll bet someone has a story that tops "I forgot to restart the app." And if this saved you an hour of panic, a ❤️ helps other beginners find it.

So, what are the best AI video generators, and how do they compare on price?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Packed with digital art tutorials, inspiration and expert advice from ImagineFX.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

We look at the hidden costs of the top 5 options.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AI video generation has matured at an astonishing speed. One minute people were mocking attempts to generate footage of Will Smith eating spaghetti, the next we have tools capable of generating footage for everything from photorealistic commercials and animated explainers to social media ads and cinematic short films, all based on simple text prompts.

But choosing the best AI video generator for you isn't only a question of visual quality. The increasing number of models available have varying limitations and strengths and weaknesses in different areas. Pricing structures can also vary wildly, and subscription fees often represent only a fraction of the cost.

Many AI video generators operate on credit systems, charge extra for premium models, or limit video length and resolution in ways that can quickly inflate your spend. With that in mind, here we take a deeper dive into the five best AI video generators available today, which we've ranked according to overall value as well as their flexibility and practical usefulness for professional creatives.

Whichever model or models you choose to work with, you'll probably want one of the best laptops for video editing or the best desktop PCs for video editors.

Adobe is already a powerhouse in filmmaking thanks to Premiere, which we rate as the best video editing software. As a result, Firefly more than just an AI video generator. Unlike many rivals that function as standalone tools, it integrates into Premiere and the wider Creative Cloud environment, making it significantly easier to incorporate AI-generated footage into existing professional workflows.

The most recent video-generation capabilities are particularly impressive. Prompt adherence is strong, footage tends to look polished and cinematic, and Adobe has invested heavily in making outputs commercially viable for professional users.

As announced at Adobe Max 2026, the native Firefly Video editor has been expanded to make it easier to combine clips with other elements such as images and sound effects, and projects created in Firefly Boards, Adobe's collaborative brainstorming space, can be carried straight into the video editor. There's also Adobe's AI agent, which makes using its software more intuitive and conversational.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

The Adobe name also offers reassurance in terms of safety and reliability. The platform's unlikely to disappear or radically change from one day to the next, and output from the native Firefly Video Models is intended to be safer for commercial use since they were trained on licensed material rather than scaped copyrighted video.

On the other hand, if you do want to use other models, they're here in Firefly itself. Google Veo, Kling, Runway and Luma AI's Ray are all integrated and ready to use in Adobe's platform, so you get not only the top AI video generator on our list but access to the second, third and fourth too!

Pricing: Adobe's Firefly Standard plan costs $9.99 per month and includes 2,000 generative credits, enough for around twenty 5-second AI video clips at 1080p quality. Higher-tier plans increase those allowances. Adobe currently prices 1080p video generation at roughly 100 credits per second, meaning a single 5-second clip consumes around 500 credits.

You can also access Firefly video generation through a Creative Cloud Pro plan, which gives you access to Adobe's suite of apps, including Premiere and Photoshop. Just note that you may need to pay for additional generative credits depending on how much footage you're generating, and partner models have different credit costs.

If pure visual quality is your priority, Google Veo has a strong claim to being the most impressive video model currently available. The platform is particularly good at realistic movement, lighting, camera behaviour and environmental detail.

Veo's ability to generate convincing cinematic scenes has made it popular among advertisers, content creators and filmmakers seeking premium-looking footage without a full production budget.

Pricing: Google offers Veo through consumer subscriptions and API access. For developers, Veo 3.1 costs roughly $0.40 per generated second, putting a typical 5-second clip at around $2.00 before retakes. That's easily the most expensive platform in this comparison, but it also delivers some of the highest-quality output available.

You can also access Veo through Google Gemini, Google AI Studio and through Adobe Firefly (see above). Through Gemini, there is a free tier offering just 3 video generations a day, at the time of writing, Google AI Pro costs $19.99 a month for 1,000 Flow credits, which translates to around 100 Lite videos or 10 high-quality Standard videos at 720p with a watermark. Google Ultra AI takes at $99.99 a month and upwards gives you 25,000 credits a month, enough to generate thousands of draft clips at 1080p.

Runway remains one of the most mature AI video platforms on the market. While some competitors may occasionally produce more realistic footage, Runway excels at putting creators in control. The platform includes advanced camera controls, editing features and creative tools that make it particularly appealing to professionals who want more than simple text-to-video generation.

The video editing features enable you to do things such as isolate a subject and remove the background, colour grading, adjusting the depth of field, making a slow motion version of your footage, and 'Inpainting', which means you can easily remove unwanted objects.

Pricing: Runway has several subscription tiers, ranging from entry-level plans through to professional and enterprise options. The credit-based system rewards efficient users but can become costly during experimentation. The Standard plan starts at $15 per month and includes 625 credits. According to Runway's published figures, that's enough for around 52 seconds of Gen-4.5 video generation. A creator producing multiple social videos every week will likely need the Pro plan at $35 per month or higher.

The Chinese platform Kling has become one of the biggest disruptors in AI video generation. It consistently produces impressive results while undercutting many western competitors on price.

Human movement, character animation and stylised content are particular strengths. For YouTubers, social creators and small businesses, Kling often represents the best balance between quality and affordability.

Pricing: Paid plans start at under $10 per month, and a 5-second 1080p clip generally consumes around 40 credits. That works out to roughly $0.40-$0.60 per clip depending on subscription tier, making Kling one of the most cost-effective premium AI video generators currently available and generally cheaper than Veo and Runway. However, as with most platforms, higher-quality generations consume credits faster.

Not every project needs blockbuster-quality visuals. Sometimes speed matters more than perfection, and that's where Hailuo shines. The platform is built around rapid iteration, allowing creators to test ideas quickly and cheaply. Agencies often use it during concept development before moving final production to more advanced systems.

Pricing: Hailuo is typically among the most affordable options available. Standard plans start between $8 and $15 per month depending on promotions and region. A typical 1080p generation consumes about 50 credits, placing the cost per clip around $0.50-$0.75.

The main trade-off is quality, and you might find yourself generating more clips to achieve a satisfactory result, which can offset the initial cost advantage. Additional editing and post-production work may also be required.

Before diving in to AI video generation, it's worth remembering that subscriptions don't always tell the whole story. A $10-per-month plan may sound inexpensive, but the number of usable clips you can generate could be surprisingly limited.

Making direct comparisons between AI video generators is difficult because some services charge per second of output, while others use credit systems that effectively works out as a cost per clip.

Pricing can also vary depending on which plan you choose and how much you use it. Heavy users are likely to have to pay extra for more credits, and plans are continually in flux. Providers have been making regular changes to their pricing as new models roll out and the competitive landscape changes. The prices being quoted today could easily change in a few weeks' time.

The challenge with any AI video generation is that creating a complete project often requires multiple generations. A single eight-second clip may be excellent, but producing several matching shots for a finished sequence can dramatically increase usage costs. You'll often need to generate dozens of variations before selecting a final clip, turning what appeared to be a modest monthly subscription into a much larger operational cost.

Users will also usually need separate editing software to assemble final productions.

For most creative professionals, we think Adobe Firefly currently represents the most balanced and versatile package while also offering reasonable value. While Google Veo may edge ahead on raw visual realism and Runway offers deeper creative controls, Firefly's integration with professional creative software makes it the most practical choice for real-world production environments, and Firefly does also provide access to Veo and Runway too.

That said, the right tool depends heavily on your needs. Consider Veo for cinematic quality, Runway for directorial control, Kling for affordability and Hailuo for rapid experimentation. Just don't judge any platform by its headline subscription price. In the AI video market, credits, render limits, premium models and editing requirements often matter far more than the monthly fee itself.

Joe is a regular freelance journalist and editor at Creative Bloq. He writes news, features and buying guides and keeps track of the best equipment and software for creatives, from video editing programs to monitors and accessories. A veteran news writer and photographer, he now works as a project manager at the London and Buenos Aires-based design, production and branding agency Hermana Creatives. There he manages a team of designers, photographers and video editors who specialise in producing visual content and design assets for the hospitality sector. He also dances Argentine tango.

Cashmere Walls, Baby Goats, and a Bruce Springsteen Sighting at Loro Piana’s Cashmere of the Year Award

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The invitation to Tuesday evening’s Loro Piana dinner arrived via courier. It was printed on heavy card stock, made out in gold ink calligraphy and sealed with red wax. It was no run-of-the-mill invite, which these days mostly arrive via email or DM, nor was it to announce the usual celebration, like a new shoe or bag. Instead, it celebrated farmers and their fibers—in particular, the farmers hailing from Mongolia who act as the conduit behind the brand’s beloved cashmere clothing and the fibers they reap from their flocks of goats in the mountains.

Now in its 11th year, the Loro Piana Cashmere Award spotlights the finest in cashmere fiber production, and that is meant in the most literal sense. At The Brant Foundation in the East Village, the first sight that greeted guests was three bundles of soft white fiber, illuminated with a dramatic spotlight. These were the winning lots selected for their superior quality, and cultivated by Mr. Siqingbilige and Mrs. Hasiqiqige, a husband-and-wife duo in first place, followed by Mr. Jiyatu and Mrs. Gegentuya in second place, and Mr. Lifuqiang and Mrs. Qiqi in third place. “The herdsmen bring us about 10 million kilos of sheared fiber, and this is the best 100 kilos, which we celebrate for their fineness and length of the fiber,” Pier Luigi Loro Piana, the son of the brand’s founder, Franco Loro Piana, told the room. “That denotes the quality.”

In addition to his role as the company’s deputy chairman, he is widely credited with creating the “baby goat cashmere” that thrust Loro Piana into the upper echelons of luxury when it was first revealed in 2008. “It took me many years to convince the herdsmen not to blend the 3% of baby cashmere fiber with the rest of the older goats,” he recalled. “It required extra work, but the difference was so clear, and it is this measure of quality that creates wealth for this population and attracts younger herdsmen to join them.”

In the room were many Loro Piana loyalists, including Huma Abedin, Gucci Westman, Jeff Koons, and Jessica Springsteen, the accomplished show jumper who serves as an ambassador for the brand’s equestrian line. She was joined by her father, the legendary rocker Bruce Springsteen, who was gleefully received by a room largely filled with Italian, French, and American guests. Evidently, the appeal of The Boss is universal.

Also on hand was Jeremy Strong, who has been outfitted by Loro Piana both on and off screen many, many times, though his first memory of the brand predates his career. “I have a memory of being in Italy and seeing the chicest man I’d ever seen in my life with two wolfhounds in a vintage Maserati convertible, and I saw the Loro Piana red mark on the back of his collar, so that image has forever emblazoned itself in the aspirational sky of my mind—just wanting to be that guy in that car,” he told Vogue.

For this occasion, Strong sported a monochromatic suit in a precise shade of Loro Piana brown-beige, and went on to tour the exhibition that had been built to highlight the farmers and their bounty. It included one room with walls of voluminous, fluffy strands of cashmere fiber which ballooned and wafted toward guests walking by. It quickly began clinging to the arms and shoulders and heads of many partygoers before an army of dashing men would offer to remove it with lint brushes with glossy tortoise handles. (In Loro Piana land, even the lint catchers are a considered detail.)

“I think what I was struck by upstairs walking through the exhibition was the way Loro Piana achieves such specificity by hand in this age of mechanical reproduction, this age where everything is computers and AI, and everything is mechanized and done by a machine,” Strong later added. “There is this care, and tactile beauty, and sort of sweat equity that goes into creating these garments. I find something very moving about that.”

The evening’s dinner—beef carpaccio, a plate of saffron risotto, and a fig tart—set the stage for further reminiscing about Loro Piana vintage finds and making plans to visit Mongolia to see the goats and the herdsmen firsthand, followed by an announcement.

After all these ceremonies celebrating the finest 100 kilos of cashmere fibers, something would finally be made using all of these fibers. “We have kept these winning fibers for the last 11 years, and we will be making a collection using them,” Frédéric Arnault, the company’s CEO, told the room. “It will be very small quantities.”

Pier Luigi Loro Piana and Laura Loro Piana

OpenAI’s personal finance features for ChatGPT expands to Mac app

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Earlier this year, OpenAI introduced ChatGPT Finances. The feature started on the mobile app and website, but it’s been missing from ChatGPT’s desktop app. Starting today. ChatGPT Finances is now available on the Mac as well.

ChatGPT Finances originally launched in beta as a preview feature for paid accounts. It originally required a $100/month plan before coming to the $20/month tier over the summer.

Since October, ChatGPT Finances has been available for all plans, including free users. The only requirement is that ChatGPT Finances is limited to users in the United States.

OpenIA says users “can securely connect your financial accounts to find ways to save, understand your spending, and make sense of your investments—with answers grounded in your finances.”
