Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

ByteDance is reportedly working on its own AI agent

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Chinese company wants to compete with Meta’s Muse.

ByteDance wants to take on Meta's Muse with its own AI agent. The company is readying an AI assistant that could be revealed in "the coming weeks," Bloomberg reports.

The report doesn't detail what the agent will be called or how it will work, but says that it will "serve a similar role" to Meta's Muse. Muse has already drawn more than 10 million users and CEO Mark Zuckerberg has predicted the AI agent could generate more revenue per user than its existing "family" of apps. The agent is also expected to play a significant role in Meta's AI glasses, and the company has previewed a standalone "charm" for Muse.

It's not clear if ByteDance is also planning on incorporating its agent into hardware. The AI assistant is one piece of the Chinese company's strategy to compete with frontier AI companies. ByteDance cofounder Zhang Yiming recently told employees that it would likely take "until at least 2029 or 2030" for the company's AI efforts to catch up, Bloomberg reported. Within the AI space, ByteDance has been primarily known for its video generator, Seedance. Clips from the video model went viral on social media platforms earlier this year, which eventually drew legal scrutiny from US-based studios.

If the TikTok maker does introduce its own AI agent, it would also likely raise new concerns about data privacy and how much access personal agents should get. ByteDance didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

For Singles Day, World explains why real is the new attractive

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Singles’ Day falls on 11 November each year and has become the world’s biggest online shopping event.

New Harris Poll research on behalf of World finds that 81% of singles would be more likely to use a dating app if every profile were confirmed as a real person.

As millions of singles celebrate Singles Day on November 11, the biggest question in online dating is no longer “are we compatible?” but “are you even real?”

A new survey conducted by The Harris Poll on behalf of World shows that trust has become the most important factor in online dating. Nearly 3 in 4 online daters (74%) say they have suspected that someone they matched with on a dating app was not a real person. Catfishing, AI-generated profiles, bots and romance scams have turned swiping into a guessing game, and singles say they are tired of it.

World’s answer is Proof of Human: a way for anyone to show they are a real, unique person online without revealing who they are.

The survey paints a clear picture of how much doubt singles now bring to every match:

In other words, being real now outranks looks. For a growing number of singles, authenticity is the new chemistry.

Singles are not giving up on dating apps. They are asking for proof. According to the research, 81% would be more likely to use a dating app if every profile were confirmed as belonging to a real person.

They are also willing to do their part. Four in five singles (80%) say they would take steps to prove there is a real person behind their own profile, as long as their matches did the same. Verification, in other words, is becoming a two-way expectation rather than a burden.

World ID is a universal set of human Proofs that lets people show they are a real, unique person online without sharing personal data such as their name, birthday or address. It is built for a world where AI can generate convincing faces, voices and conversations at scale.

The core of the system is the Orb, a spherical verification device. A person stands in front of it, and the Orb uses images of the eyes and face to confirm they are a unique, living human. That check is turned into an anonymous cryptographic credential, a verified World ID, stored in the World ID App. The process does not require a name, email or gender.

A few features make the technology a natural fit for dating:

The result is a simple promise for singles: the person on the other end of the chat is real.

The largest names in dating are moving in this direction. World first partnered with Match Group, the parent company of Tinder, on a World ID pilot in Japan in 2025. In April 2026, at World’s Lift Off event, the partnership expanded to the United States.

Tinder users who verify with World ID can display a human badge on their profile, signaling to potential matches that a verified, real person is behind it. Verification is optional.

Dating is only one use case. World ID is also being integrated into video calls, document signing, live event ticketing and gaming, with partners including Zoom and Docusign. The common thread is the same one singles describe in the survey: in the age of AI, knowing you are dealing with a real human is becoming essential.

The survey was conducted online in the United States by The Harris Poll on behalf of World from August 20 to 21, 2026, among 892 single adults aged 18 and over, of whom 649 have used online dating apps. Results are accurate to within +/- 3.6 percentage points at a 95% confidence level.

Primary Wave and Kobalt CEOs Discuss Their Billion-Dollar Partnership — And Why ‘You’ll See a Lot More’ Consolidation in Music

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the latest episode of Billboard On the Record, Larry Mestel and Laurent Hubert talk about Primary Wave's acquisition of Kobalt publicly for the first time.

In February, Billboard broke the news that Primary Wave was nearing a historic deal to acquire Kobalt, arguably the world’s largest independent frontline publisher. Together, it was estimated that the newly combined company would be worth a staggering $7 billion in assets.

By July, the deal finally closed. But given that no one from the two companies had yet spoken to the press, major questions remained about how this new arrangement would operate in practice. All that was disclosed at the time was that Kobalt would operate independently of Primary Wave, one of the largest owners of iconic music catalogs in the industry, with a roster spanning from Whitney Houston to Stevie Nicks to Bob Marley to Prince.

But would Primary Wave move all of its catalogs to Kobalt? Did the deal include assets from Kobalt’s $700m catalog fund with Morgan Stanley? And how do Kobalt’s other verticals fit into the picture? The independent publisher is also home to AMRA, a global digital rights collection service that aims to speed up royalty collection and cut out middlemen outside of the U.S., and Kosign, its digital-first publishing service for middle-class songwriters who are not ready to sign a traditional deal.

News of the acquisition also came at a time of seismic change in the music industry. Universal Music Group’s Virgin division was finalizing its acquisition of Downtown Music Holdings and BMG was moving toward its purchase of Concord, reshaping the state of independent music in a matter of months.

To explain Primary Wave and Kobalt’s new deal for the first time and why all of this consolidation is happening now, Primary Wave founder/CEO Larry Mestel and Kobalt CEO Laurent Hubert joined Billboard’s music business podcast, On the Record.

To watch this episode of Billboard On the Record, check out the YouTube video below or click here for more viewing and listening options. An edited and condensed excerpt of the conversation can also be found below.

When did you guys start discussing this deal?

Hubert: I think the beginning of the conversation was some time in the summer of 2025. I think the first conversation was not a direct conversation with me but with another party at a particular dinner.

Mestel: I remember that dinner, and Primary Wave has historically been administration-agnostic. We’re into the value creation business, not the administration business. So we’ve always used Kobalt or Universal or some other administrators instead of creating our own internal administration platform.

The dinner Laurent was speaking about was a dinner that I believe Goldman Sachs had organized. I remember at that dinner, Jenny Kim, who is head of music now at Goldman, came up to me and said, “Have you considered Kobalt as an acquisition and as a partner?” And I said, “Well, we’re administration-agnostic, but I’ve known Laurent forever. Culturally, I think we’re very similar companies.” She said, “Well, you’re going to consider it because I’m bringing you the deal.” And so I said, “Great, let’s consider it.”

At the end of the day, it made a lot of sense for us because you could never have on your team too many great managers, and Laurent’s team are fantastic managers. We saw some jewels inside of Kobalt, not just the administration platform, which is really fantastic, but divisions like AMRA and Kosign, too.

Why not do administration in-house at Primary Wave?

Mestel: For us, the focus has always been on marketing, on branding, on creating content, doing Broadway, doing movies, doing immersive experiences, creating cosmetic lines, you know, gaming, etc. So there’s a real core competency to that, and it’s very hard for other companies that are competitors to recreate what we recreate. To build an administration platform takes a significant amount of capital and takes a significant amount of effort. And we always viewed it as a commodity, where there were a lot of industry players that did administration, but not many — in fact, I can’t even name any — that does really great marketing and value creation. So we’ve always stayed away from the administration side of the business because we could always rent it at relatively insignificant cost.

You’ve said before that Kobalt is going to stand on its own, but you guys have complementary strengths. Where are the two companies going to work together?

Hubert: I have to credit Larry for the vision for the business because initially, when we first talked, I understood the interest, but I did not fully understand the vision. And then I think Larry explained that very clearly, saying, “Hey, we understand Kobalt. We understand what it means in the market and that it’s a destination today.” He says also he built a destination — which I think is very unique — and we create value differently. You know, for Primary Wave, it’s really about how do you create engagement on the properties that they acquired, and for us, it’s how do we unlock greater value from a collection perspective.

From that perspective, we saw a direct opportunity, and some of it will be that we will cross some functionality that we have, and we’ll compound those benefits without talking about merger or integration. That was also interesting for us from a management perspective because we felt we didn’t have to go through an education process about Kobalt. We don’t have to be disrupted day one. We could keep going and do what we do the best we can do.

Larry, you mentioned that there were some jewels within Kobalt that you were super interested in. You mentioned AMRA, and it’s interesting because I’ve heard similar phrasing back when Francisco Partners purchased Kobalt in 2022. They called AMRA an “unpolished gem.” So I’m curious why you think it’s a gem as well?

Mestel: Those that aren’t using AMRA are going local. How long does it take for you to collect your money? Eight months, 12 months, 15 months. AMRA is going direct, and it’s very hard to recreate AMRA because of the history of AMRA and the licenses that they’ve put in place. So, it’s touching your money sooner, and there are less people with their hands in your pocket. It’s really that simple. I think the more people discover AMRA, the more people are going to want to use AMRA. We have $7.5 billion worth of assets now within Primary Wave. We’re going to be putting as much of our repertoire through AMRA as we possibly can.

Larry, do you plan to switch all the Primary Wave catalogs over to do publishing admin through Kobalt now as well?

Mestel: We have some great partners around the world, and I’ll name two: Kobalt and Universal. We give Universal quite a bit of our administration worldwide. They are an excellent administration organization. We have a great relationship with Jody [Gerson, CEO of UMPG] and Mark [Cimino, COO of UMPG] and David [Kokakis, chief counsel of UMPG] and the team there. Kobalt will be getting much more of Primary Wave repertoire than it has gotten in the past, but we won’t be taking anything away that we’ve given Universal, and we’ll continue to give Universal more work in the future because they’ve done a great job.

I guess you also can’t just stop in the middle of an administration agreement with another company, like UMPG, just because you bought Kobalt this year.

Mestel: We have very short-term deals, but we’re not going to do that. The reason is that when you do short-term deals, you keep partners engaged. If you put up a long-term deal, maybe there’s less incentive. So I like to do month-to-month, quarter-to-quarter, year-to-year. I don’t like much more than year-to-year.

We are very well capitalized, and the fact that we’re very well-capitalized means we don’t need advances because when you take advances, that means you lower your margin. I’d rather have a much higher margin, no advance.

Luminate CEO Rob Jonas recently came on this podcast and revealed that now 150,000 songs are being uploaded to streaming services every day. With the sheer volume of music now, how has that changed your approach?

Mestel: I think that’s much more relevant for Laurent than it is for me because we are in the icon and legend business. We’re buying only the greatest of the great.

Hubert: Frankly, in the past 10-15 years, the way we manage publishing has totally transformed. We went from an ownership to a consumption model. Obviously, we went from a few hundred income sources to literally thousands of income sources.

We also have seen fragmentation of copyright. It’s not uncommon to see five, seven writers on a song, and you have fragmentation of consumption. So if you look at those factors, it has compounded volume in [many] ways, and the challenge with volume is you need to manage that with great velocity because we have to account every quarter. [Just] because you have more to manage [doesn’t mean] that you can delay your accounting period, obviously. Plus, you also have [more] challenges with accuracy. Our job as we build the infrastructure — it’s a continuous exercise — is, how do we manage this?

I’ve often had a conversation with my CTO: How do we manage [Amazon Web Services] cost? All of it is in the cloud. You have volume growing at 30%-40% a year, and you have your cost of AWS growing at 10%-12% a year, and we have to manage the business. You have to find ways to, again, optimally manage that ecosystem without affecting velocity. It’s frankly a job of every day. It really is. It’s a natural tension in the business.

So, are publishers spending more than ever to try to collect all these royalties all over the world?

Hubert: We are. The challenge is that the value of that micro license or micro transaction is so small that you have to do it cost-effectively; otherwise you’d be losing money [to collect it]. Technology is obviously a major aspect of what we do. AI is, of course, but it’s not only AI — it’s about building infrastructure that can handle the volume. It’s a challenge. This is a topic that we talk about almost every day.

Kobalt announced a $700 million fund in 2023 to buy music catalogs. Is that part of this deal, or do you retain that at Kobalt?

Hubert: We retain [it], and that’s a conversation that I had [with Larry] because we have a different IP strategy and we deploy on different type of copyrights. For us, the idea here was to provide an additional service to our clients, and we provided all the services under a lending situation. But if people needed a capital event, we wanted to be able to say yes, we can do that for you as well. We created a fund in ’11 and a second fund in ’16. We created that fund in 2023, and we’ve been deploying the capital, obviously, not only on clients that we have because we know them and they come to us, but we’re also buying third-party copyrights.

Mestel: We turn down many more deals than we do, and so Laurent’s team is participating in our deal meetings. When there’s a deal that we’re reviewing that isn’t right for Primary Wave, we’re turning it over to Kobalt [now].

Larry, I think one of the most interesting things Primary Wave has done from a marketing perspective is the Whitney Houston slot machines in Las Vegas. How are those going? Are they a success?

Mestel: It has been a giant success for us. We started this in 2024, and we budgeted 200 machines a year. Now, we’ve done over 500 a year. We just renewed the deal with [slot machine manufacturer] IGT. We are having great success, but how do you measure success? Well, you can measure success on shipping machines and shipping more than your budget, but the real measure is that we’re making more money for the estate of Whitney Houston, which we own 50% of, [on slot machines] than the entire estate was generating six and a half years ago when we bought into the estate on that one deal.

Larry, you often talk about how you guys partner with the estates when you do these deals, but I know estates can be really sensitive about certain brand partnerships. Like, anything that involves sex, alcohol or even gambling. What was that conversation like with the Houston estate to decide that this was right for her legacy?

Mestel: It was easy. Whitney loved to play slot machines. It was very organic. We will not do a brand deal, a marketing deal, any type of brand alliance, movie, anything unless the artist estate 100% signs off and says yes.

Willard Ahdritz, founder of Kobalt, is stepping away from his role as chairman as part of this deal. Who is now filling that role?

Mestel: The reality is there’s only one person that matters, which is the CEO, which is Laurent. We were clear with each other day one that the reason why Primary Wave was doing this, a big chunk of the reason, was the management team in Laurent and Kobalt. It is an entrepreneurial company. Laurent runs it with very little interference at all from Primary Wave. We talk a couple times a week, so we’re very active on what we’re doing together. Kobalt is and will remain a separate entrepreneurially-run company.

Both Primary Wave and Kobalt have active funds to buy more catalogs. Kobalt’s is a $700 million fund, announced in 2023 with Morgan Stanley, and Primary Wave’s is a $2.2 billion fund with Brookfield, announced in the spring. How much of these funds have you both deployed so far?

Mestel: We are way ahead of schedule. We’ve deployed almost 60% of our most current fund, which we only closed back in April of this year. There have been some very, very large-scale deals, and our pipeline is pretty significant. So we anticipate being probably 75% invested at the end of the year and fully invested the first quarter of next year. We have many pools of capital. We have a permanent evergreen fund with Brookfield. Brookfield put $2 billion on [the] primary balance sheet four and a half years ago, whenever that was, 2022, and we have a lot of surprises.

Hubert: We’ve deployed about half a billion dollars of the $700 million, and we intend to spend the [remainder] of that $200 million going forward. So there will be a new fund [soon] … [Primary Wave and Kobalt] don’t go after the same targets at all. We typically buy more recent vintages. We buy copyrights where we have high incentives of digital royalties because we want to take advantage of the AMRA capabilities.

2026 has been a year full of major music business acquisitions. UMG’s Virgin Music Group acquired Downtown, BMG bought Concord and Primary Wave acquired Kobalt. On a macro scale, why do you think these acquisitions and this consolidation is happening now?

Mestel: I think you can see a lot more. I think as interest rates go up, I think you will see a lot of our competitors that are financially driven looking to sell. Primary Wave, by the way, has no debt, so we’re generating significant returns without that because of the alpha we’re creating as we market. A lot of our competitors are highly leveraged, so they’re taking very safe assets that generate cash flow every single day that would normally go back out into acquisition, or go back out in the form of dividends to investors. These companies are paying interest to banks, and as interest rates go up and they have to refinance their significant debt, they’re going to have a hard time. I don’t name competitors, but you’re going to see a lot of people that are going to be selling, I think, over the course of the next two and three years. So you’ll see a lot more consolidation, and that’s great for companies like Kobalt and Primary Wave, especially ones that are very well capitalized and have almost no debt.

But is it better for artists and songwriters?

Mestel: Well, it’s better if you’re with a company that has an infrastructure to create content for them and to actually market [their music]. Is it any good when an artist is acquired by a private equity firm? Probably not, because the first thing on their mind isn’t legacy protection, and it’s not turning a hit song that was a hit 30 years ago into a hit song again. It’s, how are they going to extract as much cash out of the asset as they possibly can, and then likely flip it? So yeah, I think it’s very good if the artist is being acquired by a company that has the ability to do something.

SpaceX Wants To Build Data Center Powered By Its Newest Product: Natural Gas

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SpaceX filed its intention to build a new AI data center at its facility in Starbase, Texas, which will be powered by a new natural gas plant that it will also build. None of that sounds like space, but this is the company that SpaceX has become. The initial phase of this build-out is projected to cost $24 billion, though more phases are to come. Critically, this gives the rocket company, which is also an internet company, which is also an AI company, which is also a social media company, a way to make use of its newest product: natural gas. Again, space!

The company has been moving towards an expansion into fossil fuels for the last few months. In June, SpaceX president Gwynne Shotwell said the company was making plans for "basically mining our own natural gas... to develop our own propellant and bring it to the rocket." The rocket in question being Starship, which SpaceX is essentially betting its future on. That takes a mixture of methane and liquid oxygen to fire up its thrusters; methane can be extracted from natural gas. For that reason, the company has made major moves to ensure its own supply. It hired for its own natural gas trader, normally an oil industry job for buying and selling the stuff, and is building its own pipelines in both Florida and Texas, per Bloomberg.

In addition, SpaceX rapidly spun up AI data centers in Tennessee and Mississippi earlier this year, powered by, you guessed it, natural gas. It spun them up so rapidly that the company is now being sued for ignoring environmental laws. SpaceX is apparently trying to go fast again for this new Texas endeavor, likely trying to get things rolling before a newer, bluer state government takes over. SpaceX wants to grab both the air pollution permits it needs plus a few tax breaks; Texas is expected to make both harder to get early next year, per Politico. To that end, Starbase's city government will convene on the matter in December. There's good reason to think the company will get what it wants: two of the city's three commission members are SpaceX executives. That's handy.

As for the power plant itself, it will feature 35 mobile natural gas turbines and produce around 750 megawatts of electricity. So it's now in the business of building power plants, buying and trading natural gas, and laying out pipelines, all in service of the enough data centers it's putting up. No wonder Vice President of Launch Kiko Dontchev is now saying, "Fundamentally we are an infrastructure company at this point." You don't say, Dontchev.

At this point, it's not too much of a stretch to say that SpaceX is just the Elon Musk Show, going into whatever industry he feels like at the moment. If his long-desired merger with Tesla goes through, then it will really just be a conglomerate with all sorts of fingers in all sorts of pies. Space will still be a part of what SpaceX does, but now that AI is earning it more revenue than rockets, it may become a smaller and smaller part. Mars has never looked so far away.

Teradata: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Teradata's sovereign cloud push targets banks and governments that cannot send data to public clouds, a market where on-premise control may decide the contract.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Teradata (TDC +1.65%) raised its full-year earnings guidance and doubled its target for annual operating margin expansion. Expanding operating margins while investing in artificial intelligence (AI) software demonstrates operational discipline.

Converting customer pilot programs into recurring software revenue will determine long-term capital compounding. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Management raised full-year non-generally accepted accounting principles, or non-GAAP, earnings per share (EPS) guidance to a range of $2.86 to $2.90. Teradata also increased full-year operating margin expansion guidance. Discipline in sales and administrative expenses allowed the business to fund software research while improving profitability.

Government and banking clients increasingly require physical control over data locations to meet regulatory rules. Integrating graphics processing units, or GPUs, directly into on-premise hardware lets customers run complex analytical models without sending data to public clouds.

This is why the company launched Teradata Factory, which is a unified computing environment that brings data, large language model software, and AI agents into on-premises infrastructure to provide physical control over AI execution.

Enterprise software customers hesitate to expand automated tasks if computing costs scale out of control. Management launched Tera, an automated coworker tool for data tasks, to lower computing overhead and speed up execution.

Recurring revenue grew 4% year-to-date, providing predictable cash flows while full-year adjusted free cash flow outlook rose to $340 million at the midpoint. Control over on-premise infrastructure combined with sovereign cloud partnerships gives the company room to expand installed accounts without losing software pricing leverage.

Strategic focus remains on converting artificial intelligence experimentation into production workloads across financial services and government clients. Software flexibility positions the platform to capture complex enterprise workloads.

Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

Tether, the company behind the world's largest stablecoin, USDT, is getting surprisingly good at making tiny AI models punch above their weight.

The company on Wednesday unveiled QVAC BotPsy, an 800-million-parameter AI agent designed to carry out everyday tasks on personal devices. Parameters are essentially the knobs that determine how much an AI model can do, and more of them generally translate to improved performance. And for reference, frontier AI models today run in the trillions of parameters.

In the company's own testing, Tether’s relatively tiny model outperformed competitors with up to 2.5 times as many parameters, suggesting that AI assistants may not need enormous models to get things done.

Unlike traditional chatbots that simply respond to questions, BotPsy is agentic, meaning it’s designed to take action. It can identify which tools an application needs, execute commands in the right order, and adjust when something goes wrong.

For example, Tether described a benchmark scenario in which an AI assistant needed to send a message but encountered two problems: Wi-Fi was disabled and the inbox was full. BotPsy turned on Wi-Fi, cleared enough storage, and completed the task, while the underlying model repeatedly attempted the same failed operation.

That's a fairly mundane achievement, but it's precisely the kind of problem that makes AI agents useful outside a chatbot window.

BotPsy is built on Alibaba's Qwen3.5-0.8B architecture, a relatively small language model that Tether trained further to specialize in interacting with software tools. Its 800 million parameters represent the numerical values the model learned during training, and a smaller parameter count generally means less memory is needed to run it.

According to Tether's technical documentation, BotPsy scored 44.0% on BFCL v4, a benchmark that evaluates an AI system's ability to select and execute software functions across different tasks and conversations. Scores reflect a weighted percentage of successful performance across tool-use categories.

The underlying Qwen3.5-0.8B model scored 23.4%, while the considerably larger Qwen3.5-2B reached 37.7%.

BotPsy also scored 58.7% on ACEBench, which evaluates the accuracy of function calls, including selecting the appropriate tools and arguments. Its base model managed 30.1%, while the 2-billion-parameter competitor scored 55.4%.

The company tested five models across six tool-use evaluations, with BotPsy posting the highest results on five. Its average score across those tests reached 45.0%, compared with 38.8% for the larger Qwen model.

BotPsy is the latest addition to QVAC, Tether's effort to build an AI ecosystem that runs on devices controlled by users rather than relying entirely on centralized cloud infrastructure.

The company first introduced QVAC in May 2025, positioning the project as an alternative to AI services operated by technology giants. Its longer-term ambitions included autonomous agents capable of interacting with applications and potentially making payments using Bitcoin and USDT.

Tether expanded that initiative in October 2025 with a 41-billion-token dataset for training science and engineering models, alongside QVAC Workbench, an application that lets users run AI models locally.

Earlier this year, Tether released its SDK, providing developers with tools to build offline AI applications across mobile and desktop operating systems.

The company also developed QVAC MedPsy, a medical AI system designed to run on consumer hardware. Its 1.7-billion-parameter version outperformed Google's much larger MedGemma-27B on HealthBench Hard, a test of realistic clinical conversations evaluated using physician-developed criteria.

BotPsy is currently available as an open-weight model under the Apache 2.0 license, meaning developers can download, modify, and integrate it into their own applications. Tether has also released compressed versions intended to reduce memory requirements for local deployment.

The company has not announced a release date for a finished consumer assistant powered by BotPsy. Its published model card also says the training code and training data are not included in the release.

With $720M round, Britain’s Fractile gets a leg up in the race for AI chips

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Fractile, a British startup developing AI chips, has raised a $720 million Series C backed by Britain’s Sovereign AI Fund with hopes of “ensuring the U.K. has a greater stake in the hardware that will underpin its future economy,” its government stated.

Alongside the likes of Axelera, Etched, and OLIX, this makes Fractile one of the top-funded startup players in the race for purpose-built AI chips. Such chips are one of several avenues being explored to reduce the cost of inference — aka using increasingly demanding AI models in production.

However, Fractile seeks to go beyond hardware with a full-stack approach that also includes a machine learning compiler to maximize the speed at which LLMs will get executed on its chips.

As a result, the company frames itself as a frontier AI company, and its latest round led by Thrive Capital, Founders Fund, Lightspeed Venture Partners and Redpoint was priced accordingly. According to the British government, the valuation was “over £5 billion” — quite possibly $7 billion, a figure reported both by the Financial Times and a Reuters source.

Still, Fractile’s main differentiator will arguably be its hardware design. After a pivot, its approach now revolves on DRAM, the most common type of RAM, but stacked on top of each chip in such a way that it is hoping will bypass the usual tradeoff between memory and capacity.

This tradeoff has become more problematic with the rise of agentic AI, as companies seek to perform longer chains of tasks with large models without compromising on speed. As compute struggles to keep up, hopes are high for AI chips, despite their limited availability to date.

In a LinkedIn post, Fractile CEO Walter Goodwin wrote that “there is still a lot of work ahead” for the company, and that the new funding will enable the startup to “ship [its] first chips and systems next year.”

Bose recalls 670,000 Bluetooth speaker batteries due to fire risk — what to do now

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Our weekly newsletter is expertly crafted to immerse you in the world of streaming. Stay updated on the latest releases and our top recommendations across your favorite streaming platforms.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Bose has issued a recall of more than 670,000 lithium-ion battery packs used in the company's S1 Pro and S1 Pro+ Bluetooth speaker systems due to overheating issues.

According to a Consumer Product Safety Commission announcement, Bose has received reports of the battery packs overheating to the point of melting, producing smoke or catching fire. Fortunately, no injuries have been reported.

The recall specifically affects battery packs that include any one of the following six digits in the serial numbers: 083723, 078352, 084105, 078591, 078592, 084106, or 086409. You can find the 17-digit number on the label printed on the bottom of the pack.

These packs were sold both with the S1 Pro and S1 Pro+ speakers as well as accessories, for between $100 and $160 from December 2017 through July 2026. The speaker systems cost $700.

In total, 634,759 were sold in the US, with an additional 35,400 in Canada.

If you have the S1 Pro or S1 Pro+ Portable Bluetooth Speaker Systems, Bose requests that you stop using the battery packs immediately and remove them from the speakers. You can still use the speakers connected to AC power.

If you have an uninstalled battery pack, you just need to find the serial number and send it to Bose.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Bose is asking owners to register and provide the serial number at http://www.bose.com/support/s1-battery-recall. The company is providing a free replacement battery pack.

You'll need to dispose of the battery safely, which means do not just throw the battery pack in the trash or in used battery recycling boxes at retail or home improvement stores. Lithium-ion batteries must be disposed of differently from other batteries because of the greater risk of fire.

You'll need to look up your local municipality's household hazardous waste collection center. You should contact the center because not everyone accepts lithium-ion batteries. They will generally be able to point you in the right direction.

United Airlines CEO Scott Kirby Bans AI Agents From Booking Tickets, Doesn’t Want “Another Middleman”

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

United Airlines is banning personal AI assistants from booking flights. Its new contract of carriage goes beyond United.com to agents working through travel agencies, and applies even when a customer supervises or approves what the software does. The airline cites privacy, unintended purchases and regulatory requirements. But CEO Scott Kirby revealed the real reason at an event in Chicago. “To be honest, I don’t want to create another middleman.” I get that they want to negotiate the terms before a new company gets between United and its customers. But United can’t just decide that customers will have to do the work themselves and expect that all customers will go ahead and do the work to buy tickets from United when it becomes easier just to buy from someone else. And writing a ban into a contract doesn’t mean United can actually identify an AI booking that comes through Expedia. The Ban Reaches Beyond United’s Website United’s November 11 contract of carriage revision adds AI agents to its prohibited ticketing practices. Rule 6.J.6 bans: The use of AI Agents to make, modify or service a Ticket, and/or to purchase any service or product. The definition includes assistants accessing United through a travel agency or another intermediary. Human supervision or approval doesn’t circumvent that. And its Rule 5.H gives United the right to cancel reservations made, modified or serviced by these agents. The Wall Street Journal says agents can still present United flights and prices. AI research, followed by the customer making a purchase on their own, doesn’t run afoul of United’s rules. United Wouldn’t Know If An AI Agent Bought Through Expedia? Expedia already has a ChatGPT integration. It returns flight and hotel options with prices and availability. Expedia’s current instructions then send the customer to Expedia to complete the purchase. It’s not an autonomous purchase inside ChatGPT, but if an agent completes the purchase on Expedia United just sees an Expedia booking not how the booking was made. United says its systems can identify booking sources. Sure, but that is a non-sequitur. A contractual ban isn’t a technical one. United may push intermediaries to enforce the restriction, but there are lots of intermediaries out there. But we’ve seen airlines go to war with online travel agencies before, cutting off their own sales. The Strongest Case For United Is About Bargaining Power United may want to limit access to its sysems: The next middleman could become expensive. An assistant that decides which flights customers see can eventually demand referral fees, sell placement or favor suppliers that agree to its terms. Sam Altman says that if an AI does this, no one will trust it, and the stakes are too high to compromise trust for a travel commission. Someone has to own the mistakes. An agent can buy the wrong dates, misunderstand a fare restriction or cancel a ticket the customer wanted to keep. Agencies have commercial arrangements for ticketing and servicing. An AI assistant operating a browser may not bring the same accountability. And the law is evolving here. I’ve covered Delta’s own AI canceling a return flight it said it would preserve. Delta acknowledged a bug and restored the itinerary. Shopping consumes resources even when nobody buys. An agent can compare far more combinations than a human. The International Air Transport Association has examined the cost of growing search volumes. But United isn’t banning search, just completing the transaction. United can do this now, but it won’t last. The airline says agent bookings are still rare. That makes this a relatively inexpensive time to withhold permission. Every customer who gets used to buying another airline through an assistant becomes harder to win back. And the same customers most likely to use an AI assistant are the ones most likely to come over to United for Starlink. United has a hub in San Francisco. It’s ultimately not going to work for Scott Kirby to stand athwart history yelling “stop!” Customers Have A Reason To Hire The Bot United’s safety argument is weak. The federal hazardous-materials rule requires passenger notices, including at ticket purchase where practical and at check-in, and passenger acknowledgment in self-service transactions. It doesn’t require the customer to manually complete every other step of a booking. An assistant can stop and ask the passenger to review a disclosure or give a required confirmation. United’s definition still covers an agent whose actions the customer supervises and approves. Nor does accepting an agent have to mean giving unfamiliar software unrestricted use of a MileagePlus password. Visa is developing ways for merchants to recognize approved agents and connect their actions with customer and payment information. The customer’s time is part of the cost of buying a flight. If I can tell an assistant my dates, preferred airports, seat requirements and budget, there’s value in having it compare the choices and handle the purchase. Sometimes United’s schedule will be good enough that consumers will do the extra work. Sometimes another airline will offer a comparable trip and let an assistant finish it. We’ve already seen an airline overestimate its ability to make customers use its preferred distribution channels. American backed away from its plan to deny mileage earning on certain travel agency bookings. Customers didn’t all abandon the agencies and processes they preferred just because American wanted them to. There’s also an obvious reason airlines might dislike an assistant that works well for the passenger. It can compare the total price, recognize benefits the customer already gets from status or a credit card, and decline add-ons that aren’t worth buying. United has the worst value prop of any U.S. airline for basic economy! I’ve made the same argument about rewards credit cards: companies are excited about using AI to sell more, but customers get better tools too. The definition includes assistants accessing United through a travel agency or another intermediary. Human supervision or approval doesn’t circumvent that. And its Rule 5.H gives United the right to cancel reservations made, modified or serviced by these agents.

18 leaders on supporting diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The workplace doesn’t work equally well for everyone. Some workers face obvious physical challenges that require accommodation. Others have diverse physical, cognitive, and learning needs that can stay hidden, overlooked, or brushed aside. When those needs are unmet, workers can struggle to do their best work. When their needs are understood and addressed, workers can flourish and so can the workplace.

We asked members of the Fast Company Impact Council what workplaces should consider doing to better support people with diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs. Here, 18 of those leaders share insights on what does and doesn’t help when it comes to supporting all people.

1. DESIGN WITH PEOPLE AND LIVED EXPERIENCE

The goal should be to design with people, not simply for them. Lived experience can reveal barriers that may be invisible to those creating the workplace and, when incorporated into the design, often leads to solutions that are more intuitive, flexible, and effective for everyone. That means involving people with diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs in shaping the workplace itself from the beginning—from the physical environment and technology to communication, policies, and organizational practices. Accessibility should not be an afterthought but a fundamental design principle. — Gail Zauder, AbleFly Inc.

2. MAKE COMMUNICATIONS ADJUSTMENTS

I have an employee with autism, and she has taught me a great deal about how differently people process information. When I provide clear direction, specific responsibilities, and complete expectations, she performs exceptionally well. I’ve learned to be patient and adapt my communication style to what works best for her. She is genuinely one of my best employees and consistently exceeds expectations. As leaders, we need to recognize that everyone works and learns differently. Sometimes small adjustments in how we communicate make an extraordinary difference. — Kim Wileman, No Makeup Makeup

3. DON’T MAKE PEOPLE DISCOVER THEIR ACCOMMODATIONS

Proactivity and clarity are a great starting place. Our Neurodiversity at Work Survey found that 70% of neurodivergent employees don’t know what accommodations they’re entitled to, and 60% don’t know who to ask. Leaders can change that by making accommodations known from the start, rather than something employees have to uncover or disclose to get. Tell candidates what’s available during interviews, when they join, and throughout their tenure. Flexible workspaces, meeting pre-reads, and transcription tools can make a big difference. These are things that benefit everyone and are especially helpful to those with cognitive differences. — Nathan Friedman, Understood.org

4. UPDATE POLICIES AND LISTEN

Most policies exist because of something that happened before there was a policy. So when a new hire asks why something works the way it does and the answer is “that’s how it’s always been,” that’s a good sign it’s time for an update. Beyond that, listen. Not a survey whose feedback sits untouched for months, but active listening in one-to-ones, Slack channels, team meetings, and the break room. A generic response that ignores what people said is worse than no response at all. So listen, then be clear about what can change, what can’t, and why. — Colby Proffitt, Seekr

5. DESIGN WITH FLEXIBILITY FROM THE START

Most workplaces were designed around a narrow definition of how people should think, communicate, and perform. That model is outdated. The better approach is to design for flexibility from the start, rather than forcing people to ask for exceptions just to do their best work. — Ran Mullins, Psympl

6. ASK PEOPLE WHAT HELPS THEM DO THEIR BEST WORK

Ask people what they need to do their best work, then listen. It’s harder than it sounds and worth the effort. Share agendas ahead of time, let people weigh in live or afterward, and caption and record meetings. One of my team’s culture intentions is “Clear is Kind.” Clarity takes effort and sometimes courage, and it drives better outcomes. — Michele Walsh, UNICEF USA

7. RETHINK WHO GETS TO LEAD

Living with a vision disability has taught me that systems designed for the “average” person leave talent behind. That insight fuels my commitment to inclusive leadership, and my belief that accessibility and inclusion are drivers of innovation. Across education, technology, executive search, and leadership advisory, we need to challenge organizations to rethink who gets to lead, how leadership is defined, and how different perspectives create stronger organizations. Difference is a strength, not a barrier. — Meredith Rosenberg, NU Advisory Partners

8. MAKE IT COMFORTABLE TO ASK FOR HELP

I think workplaces should spend less time trying to make everyone work the same way. People think, learn, communicate, and solve problems differently, and that can be a real strength. Leaders should be clear about the outcome they need while giving people flexibility in how they get there. It’s also important to create an environment where people are comfortable asking for what they need without feeling singled out. Some of the best ideas come from people who see a problem differently than everyone else. If we build workplaces around only one way of thinking or working, we shouldn’t be surprised when we get the same kinds of answers. — Yuping Huang, Quantum Computing Inc.

9. MAKE FLEXIBILITY THE DEFAULT

Workplaces are strongest when they embrace diverse perspectives, experiences, and ways of thinking. Innovation often comes from people who see challenges differently. By fostering an open-minded, inclusive environment and providing flexibility for different problem-solving approaches, organizations can unlock new possibilities and empower everyone to contribute at their highest potential. — Chris K Bailey, Bailey Brand Consulting

10. LOOK AT CULTURE AND POLICY TOGETHER

First, people with diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs bring real strengths. Many colleagues with dyslexia or ADHD are standout big picture thinkers, and colleagues with autism often catch patterns others miss. Next, look at culture and policy together. Do your rigid schedules, meeting norms, or accommodation process bring out their best, or get in the way? Written agendas and flexible hours go a long way. Finally, partner with Understood.org, 2Gether-International, and Easterseals, whose research and expertise can help everyone contribute at their best. — Muneer Panjwani, Engage for Good | The Halo Awards

11. FOCUS ON HOW PEOPLE LEARN

Having someone on our team with severe depression made us rethink our interview process and how we work. We focused more on how people learn and apply what they know and asked what support they need to do that well. Workplaces should give people different ways to demonstrate their skills and flexibility in how they do the work. That can mean written instructions, accessible tools, or time to process information before responding. Ask the person, then follow through. — Andra Vaduva, SafeSpace

12. ADJUSTMENTS SHOULD BE STANDARD PRACTICE

Start by asking people how they work best and then action the answer. Intentional process can cover most of what is typically needed: agendas sent before meetings, written follow-ups after them, captions turned on, and a choice between written and spoken updates. These changes cost almost nothing and help everyone, including people who will never ask. Adjustments can be standard practice for the whole team. — James Chester, WVN

13. MAKE ROOM FOR DIFFERENCES

Make flexibility a core part of your workplace culture. Some people hit their productivity stride at 6 a.m., others at 4 p.m. Some respond quickly and others need time to digest the information for a bit before offering an idea that strengthens the plan. Some work better in silence, others with background noise or music in their headphones. Don’t put roadblocks in front of talent by thinking there’s only one way of working. Make room for those differences so everyone has an opportunity to do their best work. Make sure people feel comfortable requesting the tools or accommodations they need to thrive. — Matt Mandrella, City of Huntsville, AL

14. UNDERSTAND HOW YOUR PEOPLE WORK

The best place to start is recognizing that there is no single way people do their best work. Some need schedule flexibility, some process information differently, and others need different tools or environments to stay focused. That puts responsibility on managers to understand how their people work and create room for those differences. It should be easy for an employee to say, “Here’s what helps me do my best work,” without feeling they have to justify it. When you give people the flexibility to play to their strengths, you build a better workplace for everyone. — Matt Conlin, Fluent

15. DON’T TREAT FLEXIBILITY LIKE AN EXCEPTION

Workplaces should start by making flexibility normal instead of treating it like an exception. That means offering different ways to communicate, contribute, process information, and structure work. The goal should be to judge people by the quality of their output, not by whether they work in the exact same style as everyone else. — George Kailas, Prospero.ai

16. IDENTIFY BARRIERS AND DEVELOP PRACTICAL SOLUTIONS

I believe workplaces should focus on adapting their environments, communication, and training to different cognitive, physical, and learning needs. This matters because providing the same resources to everyone is not always enough. Employees may need accessible technology, flexible schedules, or different ways to receive information and demonstrate their abilities. Businesses should involve their teams in identifying barriers and developing practical solutions. Today, workplace inclusion should not be treated simply as a compliance issue. It should be part of how organizations develop talent and support performance. — Manuel Freire-Garabal, Gioya HEI

17. DON’T WAIT FOR AN ACCOMMODATION ASK

The best workplaces don’t wait for someone to ask for an accommodation. They design the workplace with differences in mind, recognizing that people process information, learn, and communicate in unique ways. When we reduce barriers, we enable people to spend less energy navigating the workplace and more energy contributing their talents, hopefully making the workplace better for everyone. — Namrata Yadav, Truist Foundation

18. FOSTER A CULTURE OF EMPATHY AND OPEN ACCESS

Supporting diverse needs requires shifting from only reactive accommodations to intentional workplace design. At The Trevor Project, accessibility is central to our core value of belonging. Part of why we have embraced remote work is that we’ve learned it is invaluable for accommodating disabilities, chronic illness, and learning differences. While no organization can proactively anticipate every requirement, leaders must foster a culture of empathy and open access where employees feel comfortable and empowered to request the individualized support they need to thrive. — Jaymes Black, The Trevor Project

iCloud+ has new perk in iOS 27 if you pay for more storage

With Apple Intelligence in the Home app, you can automatically generate video descriptions for recorded clips from compatible Home cameras. You can then search your recordings using natural language

London’s Fractile raises $720M for memory-first AI chips and hits $7B valuation

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

London-based chip designer Fractile has raised $720 million in a Series C round, betting that memory bandwidth, rather than raw computing power, is the main constraint holding back AI performance.

The funding values the four-year-old company at $7 billion, according to the Financial Times, which cited people familiar with the deal.

Thrive Capital, Founders Fund, Lightspeed, and Redpoint led the round. Other investors include Alphabet’s CapitalG, DST Global, BlackRock, Bain Capital Ventures, T. Rowe Price, Shopify Ventures, Balderton, the UK’s Sovereign AI fund, and the British Business Bank.

The valuation is a sharp jump from May, when Fractile raised around $220 million at a valuation of roughly $1 billion. The new figure puts it among the UK’s most valuable startups, alongside companies such as Revolut.

The Financial Times also reports that Anthropic has expressed interest in becoming an early customer for Fractile’s chips, citing people familiar with the discussions.

Founder Walter Goodwin’s argument starts with a growing imbalance between computing power and memory bandwidth.

Over the past 25 years, chip performance has increased roughly a millionfold, while memory bandwidth has grown only about 40 times. That leaves a 25,000-fold gap between the amount of data processors can calculate and how quickly they can access it.

Goodwin told the FT that today’s frontier models are “drinking through a straw”.

Fractile’s proposed solution combines custom DRAM and logic in a three-dimensional stack. The company says this architecture can deliver substantially higher bandwidth than high-bandwidth memory (HBM), while retaining enough capacity to run large models with long context windows. Goodwin claims it could make inference, the process of generating responses to user queries, up to 10 times faster.

Those performance gains have yet to be demonstrated in a production chip. Fractile has not released a commercial product, and its first chip is planned for next year. For now, the $7 billion valuation is based on its technology and prototypes rather than a product in the market.

The company is also trying to avoid a growing constraint in the AI supply chain. Many competing chipmakers rely on HBM to move data quickly between memory and processors.

The technology is expensive, and demand from AI companies is putting pressure on suppliers such as SK Hynix and Micron.

Fractile is entering an increasingly crowded market. Nvidia acquired Groq last year and launched its own inference chip in March, while startups including Etched, MatX, and fellow British company Olix are developing alternatives.

The opportunity, according to Thrive Capital’s Philip Clark, is an inference market that could soon become “at least as large as the market Nvidia is addressing today”.

He sees particular potential in AI agents, which need to run continuously for hours or days rather than respond to a single prompt.

The funding is also a significant moment for the UK’s effort to build domestic AI infrastructure. Goodwin described the government’s Sovereign AI fund as a driving force behind developing frontier AI technology from London.

Fractile also has offices in the US and Taiwan, however, and is building for a global market. Its next challenge is to turn its prototypes into working chips that can deliver the performance it has promised.

I am the Editor in Chief for TNW, covering technology not as a parade of launches and valuations, but as a system of influence, persuasion, (show all) I am the Editor in Chief for TNW, covering technology not as a parade of launches and valuations, but as a system of influence, persuasion, and change. I write about startups, venture capital, digital policy, and Europe ecosystem, with an eye on the larger story beneath them: who gets to build the future, who profits from it, and how Europe is learning to speak in a louder voice of its own. Before moving into senior editorial leadership, I've built my career for over +10 years across journalism, storytelling, content strategy, SEO, and digital publishing, with experience in SaaS, hospitality, art, and culture.
